\[
\kappa(r) \;=\; \frac{(r-1)^{2}}{r\ln r},
\qquad \kappa(2) = 0.72135,\quad \kappa(3) = 1.21365 .
\]
Holding the field layout fixed, moving the scale from $r=2$ to $r=3$ therefore
multiplies the error by $\kappa(3)/\kappa(2) = 1.6825$ while multiplying the
dynamic range by $\log_2 3 = 1.5850$. Measured in positions, it costs
$\log_2 1.6825 = 0.7506$ of precision and buys $\log_3 1.5850 = 0.4192$ of range:
a net loss of $0.3314$ positions per number. Under this model, and counting in
abstract radix positions rather than in packed binary storage, a power-of-two
scale therefore has the lower predicted error at equal position count. The
comparison is not a statement about packed width on a binary fabric: by
Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} a trit-indexed field must additionally pay
$\lceil E_t\log_2 3\rceil$ bits of storage, and that cost is accounted separately
in Theorem~\ref{thm:boundary}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The quantisation step over $[1,r)$ is $\Delta = (r-1)2^{-M}$. Under
round-to-nearest the signed error is uniform on $(-\Delta/2, \Delta/2)$, so the
mean \emph{absolute} error is $\Delta/4$ --- a quarter of the step, not half of
it, which is the maximum rather than the mean. On a log-uniform workload the
significand has density $1/(s\ln r)$ on $[1,r)$, so
$\mathbb{E}[1/s] = \frac{1}{\ln r}\int_1^{r} s^{-2}\,ds = \frac{1-1/r}{\ln r}$.
Multiplying gives $\tfrac14 (r-1)2^{-M}\cdot\frac{1-1/r}{\ln r}$, and
$(r-1)(1-1/r) = (r-1)^2/r$, which is $\tfrac14\kappa(r)2^{-M}$ as stated. For
the range, $E_t$ trits span $3^{E_t}$ exponent states --- $3^{E_t}-2$ of them
ordinary in TNF, the two extremes being reserved --- of $\log_2 r$ binades each. One
mantissa bit halves the error and one exponent trit triples the range, which
fixes the exchange rate between the two currencies and gives the position
figures.
\end{proof}

Direct measurement at two rungs, encoding and decoding through both scales in
exact arithmetic, gives error ratios of $1.675$ at $(E_t,M) = (4,11)$ and $1.690$
at $(6,25)$ against the predicted $1.6825$, and range ratios of $1.583$ and
$1.585$ against the predicted $1.5850$.

\paragraph{The prefactor was wrong by a factor of four, and only the prefactor.}
An earlier statement of this theorem gave the expected error as $\kappa(r)2^{-M}$,
because the proof took the mean rounding error to be half a step rather than a
quarter and then absorbed a second factor of two into a convention. A Monte-Carlo
check of $8\times10^{6}$ log-uniform draws returns $7.0471\times10^{-4}$ at
$(r,M)=(2,8)$ against $\tfrac14\kappa(2)2^{-8} = 7.0444\times10^{-4}$, and
$1.4822\times10^{-4}$ at $(3,11)$ against $\tfrac14\kappa(3)2^{-11} =
1.4815\times10^{-4}$, so the corrected prefactor is confirmed to four figures
\textbf{[measured --- software]}. Nothing downstream moves: every use of $\kappa$
elsewhere in this paper is a \emph{ratio}, and $\kappa(3)/\kappa(2) = 1.6825$ is
unchanged by a common factor, as is the minimisation of $\kappa$ over the
shift-only set in Theorem~\ref{thm:radixopt}. This is recorded rather than
silently corrected because an absolute constant that no ratio depends on is
exactly the kind of number a reader will lift out of context.

\begin{corollary}[What TNF is, precisely]
TNF is not a ternary float in the sense of Ternary27; it is a binary-radix float
whose exponent is \emph{encoded} in balanced ternary. Theorem~\ref{thm:scaleradix}
says that under its log-uniform, fixed-layout model this choice has the lower
predicted relative error of the two available ones, by $0.331$
position-equivalents per number, and Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} says that the ternary encoding pays only on a
ternary fabric. The two together delimit the format exactly: the radix choice is
right on any fabric, and the encoding choice is right on one.
\end{corollary}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-59-stateslotledger.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Ternary is more economical in state-slots, but binary packing restores the cost and yields equal mantissa widths in the named tested cases.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-59stateslotledger}
\end{figure}



\section{The regime field is a universal code}
\label{sec:codes}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-14-codes.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The regime field as a prefix code (this section): the unary and logarithmic staircases, and the Kraft floor no format of unbounded range can taper beneath.}
\label{fig:canon-codes}
\end{figure}


Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy} identifies the exponent's encoding as a prefix code.
That identification is worth making explicit, because it places the taxonomy of
Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy} inside a hierarchy that predates every format in the
catalogue by decades~\cite{elias1975}.

\begin{theorem}[The staircase is the codeword-length function]
\label{thm:code}
Let a format of $N$ positions encode its exponent with a prefix code assigning
$e$ a codeword of length $\ell(e)$. Then
$M_{\mathrm{eff}}(e) = N - 1 - \ell(e)$, and the staircase form is the growth
rate of $\ell$ and nothing else.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The sign consumes one position and the exponent prefix consumes $\ell(e)$,
leaving $N-1-\ell(e)$ positions for the significand.  This is exactly the
effective mantissa at exponent $e$.  As $|e|$ grows, the only varying term is
$\ell(e)$, so the staircase is its growth rate, with the sign and the fixed
word width contributing only a constant offset.
\end{proof}

The catalogue's three tapering classes are then three points in the classical
hierarchy: a unary regime, $\ell \propto |e|$, gives posit's arithmetic
staircase; a length-prefixed binary regime, $\ell \propto \log_2|e|$, gives
takum's geometric one; a fixed field, $\ell = \mathrm{const}$, gives no staircase
at the price of bounded support. Elias' $\gamma$, $\delta$ and $\omega$ codes
occupy the logarithmic class at successively finer asymptotics.

\begin{theorem}[Logarithmic is the floor]
\label{thm:floor}
For any prefix code on the integers, $\ell(e) \geq \log_2|e|$ for infinitely many
$e$. Hence no format of unbounded range tapers more gently than one bit per
doubling of $|e|$, asymptotically.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Kraft's inequality gives $\sum_e 2^{-\ell(e)} \leq 1$. If $\ell(e) < \log_2|e|$
for all but finitely many $e$, the tail of the sum exceeds
$\sum_e 1/|e|$, which diverges.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:floor} is worth stating plainly because it bounds this work as
much as its competitors: takum's regime is asymptotically optimal, and no format
--- TNF included --- can beat it over unbounded range. What TNF does instead is
decline the unbounded range, which Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy} shows is the only
other option. The accuracy advantages Section~\ref{sec:accuracy} once claimed inside that
range did not survive their own correction: with the takum oracle's negation
defect fixed and the stored widths equalised, the accumulation measurement is
a three-way tie among TNF(4,8), takum16 and true tekum, no format winning more
than the width it gives up. What holds inside and outside the range alike is
structural --- the fixed field has no regime codec and a $2.98\times$ smaller
adder --- and that, not accuracy, is the claim this theorem leaves standing.

\begin{theorem}[The regime's radix does not matter]
\label{thm:radixinv}
A regime spending one digit per multiplication of $|e|$ by $r$, over an alphabet
of $r$ symbols, costs $\log_2 r \cdot \log_r |e| = \log_2 |e|$ bits, independent
of $r$. The staircase form is invariant to the regime's radix.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The number of radix-$r$ digits needed to distinguish magnitudes through $|e|$
is $\log_r|e|$ (with the usual ceiling when only integral code lengths are
allowed).  A digit from an $r$-symbol alphabet carries $\log_2r$ bits when
stored in binary.  Therefore the continuous code-length cost is
$\log_2r\,\log_r|e|=(\log_2r)(\log_2|e|/\log_2r)=\log_2|e|$.
The ceiling contributes only the endpoint staircase, so it changes the
rounding of the same logarithmic curve, not its radix-independent form.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:radixinv} retracts a construction we proposed in an earlier draft
of this work. Having observed the gap between the arithmetic and geometric
classes, we suggested a regime spending one trit per tripling of $|e|$ as the
natural way for a ternary format to occupy it. It does not occupy it: building
both codes and normalising each to satisfy Kraft, the two codeword-length
functions differ by an additive constant of exactly $1$ at every $|e|$ from $1$ to
$4096$. One trit per tripling is takum's staircase with a different constant, not
a third form. A ternary fabric changes what the regime costs to decode; it does
not change what it costs to encode.

\paragraph{The gap is real, occupiable, and empty for a reason we can only
conjecture.} Growth rates strictly between $\log_2|e|$ and $|e|$ do exist and are
Kraft-feasible --- $\ell(e) = \lceil\sqrt{|e|}\rceil$ is one --- and such a code
is not dominated. At $N = 16$, normalised so that all three are legal prefix
codes:

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{$M_{\mathrm{eff}}$ by $|e|$ and usable range for three regime codes at
$N = 16$, each normalised to satisfy Kraft. The square-root code is a genuine
Pareto point: more precision than the logarithmic code throughout the core, more
range than the unary one.}
\label{tab:codes}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}
\toprule
regime & $|e|{=}1$ & $4$ & $16$ & $64$ & $256$ & binades \\
\midrule
unary, $\ell \propto |e|$ (posit class)      & $11$ & $10$ & $7$ & $-5$ & $-53$ & $43$ \\
$\ell = \lceil\sqrt{|e|}\rceil$              & $11$ & $10$ & $8$ & $4$  & $-4$  & $121$ \\
$\ell \propto \log_2|e|$ (takum class)       & $9$  & $7$  & $5$ & $3$  & $1$   & $511$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

For reference, a fixed field reaching the same 121 binades as the square-root code
spends 8 bits on the exponent and holds $7$ bits flat everywhere --- better than
the square-root code beyond $|e| \approx 30$ and worse below it, which is the
taper trade in miniature.

So the gap is not empty because its occupants are dominated; they are not. Our
first explanation was decode cost, and building the hardware refutes it.
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-71-boundedalternati.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Beyond the central codeword interpretation, the section retracts a ternary-regime advantage, limits fixed-field accuracy claims to TNF's range, and locates the logarithmic class among Elias codes.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-71boundedalternati}
\end{figure}


\subsection{What a regime costs in fabric}\label{sec:regimecost}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-36-regimecost.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Three regime codecs on one part: the length-prefixed code decodes in 6 LUTs at 805.8 MHz, and a fixed field has no regime to decode at all.}
\label{fig:canon-regimecost}
\end{figure}


We wrote three regime codecs to one interface --- a 16-bit word in, an exponent
and a mantissa shift out, and the mirror encoder --- and synthesised each under a
single harness on XC7A200T with Yosys and nextpnr-xilinx, hard multipliers
disabled. These are structural models of the three classes, not reproductions of
the published posit or takum codecs; what they compare is a run-scanned regime
against a length-prefixed one, which is the structural difference the taxonomy
names.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{Regime codec cost by class, post-route on XC7A200T under one harness,
no DSP. Figures are net of the harness, which costs $24$ LUTs and was synthesised
separately and subtracted; the round-trip column is decode plus encode.}
\label{tab:regimecost}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
\toprule
class & \multicolumn{2}{c}{decode} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{encode} & round trip \\
      & LUTs & $F_{\max}$ & LUTs & $F_{\max}$ & LUTs \\
\midrule
unary, $\ell \propto |e|$ (posit)          & $379$ & $36.8$\,MHz  & $59$  & $211.2$\,MHz & $438$ \\
$\ell \propto \log_2|e|$ (takum)           & $\mathbf{6}$ & $\mathbf{805.8}$\,MHz & $\mathbf{34}$ & $\mathbf{285.1}$\,MHz & $\mathbf{40}$ \\
$\ell = \lceil\sqrt{|e|}\rceil$            & $393$ & $33.1$\,MHz  & $123$ & $96.4$\,MHz  & $516$ \\
\midrule
fixed field (GF, TNF)                     & $0$   & ---          & $0$   & ---          & $\mathbf{0}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The conjecture said the square-root code would be too expensive to be worth
building. It is not: it costs $18\%$ more than the unary regime round trip, and
the unary regime ships in production. What the post-route result does show is that the
expense lies somewhere else than we assumed, and this has a closed form.

\begin{theorem}[Decode cost is set by the scan, not by the staircase]
\label{thm:scan}
A regime whose codeword length is delimited by a run must examine up to $N-1$
positions to decode, giving cost $\Theta(N)$. A regime whose length is carried in
a fixed field of $b$ bits requires no scan and a shift of at most $2^{b}-1$,
giving cost $\Theta(1)$ in $N$. The two costs are independent of the growth rate
of $\ell$, and therefore of the staircase form.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A run-delimited regime can terminate only after the decoder has inspected the
run, and a legal word may place that terminator at the last of its $N-1$
non-sign positions.  The worst-case scan is therefore $N-1$, while the
corresponding sequential implementation supplies a linear upper bound: the
cost is $\Theta(N)$.  A fixed field has constant width $b$ and a fixed
location, so extraction and a shift by at most $2^b-1$ take a number of steps
independent of $N$, namely $\Theta(1)$.  Neither bound depends on the
particular growth law of $\ell$.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:scan} predicts the table exactly. The unary and square-root codes
share the same 15-position scan and cost $403$ and $417$ LUTs, a difference of
$3.5\%$ that is the squarer and nothing else; the length-prefixed code has no scan
and costs $30$. The square-root code is geometrically intermediate and structurally
identical to the unary one, which is the point: \emph{the staircase form and the
decode cost are independent axes}, and a taxonomy of one does not predict the
other.

\begin{corollary}[In this structural-codec harness the logarithmic regime is not
a trade]
\label{cor:takumwins}
Against the unary regime the length-prefixed one has the better figure on each of
the three axes this harness measures --- and only those three; energy, full
datapath latency and area on other devices were not measured:
asymptotically optimal by Theorem~\ref{thm:floor}, $11\times$ smaller round trip,
and $22\times$ faster to decode. The published bounded-regime posit
variants~\cite{positgap}, which cap the regime field precisely to avoid the scan,
are the same conclusion reached from the other direction.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The structural part follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:floor}: a logarithmic
length-prefixed code meets the Kraft lower bound, whereas the unary code does
not.  The measured round-trip costs in Table~\ref{tab:regimecost} are
$438$ and $40$ LUTs, respectively, so their ratio is
$438/40=10.95$, which rounds to the stated $11\times$.  The corresponding
decode frequencies are $36.8$ and $805.8$\,MHz; their ratio
$805.8/36.8=21.90$ gives the stated $22\times$ frequency advantage.  These
are measurements of this harness and device, not claims about energy or an
arbitrary datapath.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The post-route price of unbounded range]
\label{cor:price}
A fixed field has no regime codec at all. The dichotomy of
Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy} therefore has a hardware statement: unbounded range
costs $40$ LUTs at best and $438$ at worst on this device, against $0$ for a
format that declines it. Section~\ref{sec:convert} converts those LUTs into the
same currency as the accuracy results, which is the only way to say whether they
matter.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A fixed-field format reads its exponent and significand from fixed positions and
has no regime codec, so its regime contribution is $0$ LUTs by construction.
For the two tapered codec models, Table~\ref{tab:regimecost} records the
measurements $40$ and $438$ LUTs on the XC7A200T; their minimum and maximum are
therefore those two values. These numbers are measurements, not consequences of
Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy}; that theorem supplies only the structural comparison.
Thus the stated range of measured regime costs follows without turning a device
measurement into an algebraic prediction.
\end{proof}

We are left without an explanation for the empty gap. It is not domination, since
the square-root code is Pareto-undominated; and it is not cost, since it is within
$16\%$ of a regime that ships. Both of our explanations are now measured and both
are wrong, and we record that rather than replace it with a third.


\section{Converting fabric area into precision}\label{sec:convert}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-15-convert.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Converting fabric area into precision (this section): the marginal area of a mantissa bit, the exchange rate it defines, and the resulting bill for the regime field.}
\label{fig:canon-convert}
\end{figure}


Corollary~\ref{cor:price} gives a regime's cost in LUTs and Section~\ref{sec:accuracy}
gives an accuracy ratio, and the two cannot be compared until they are in one
currency. They can be: at a fixed class, a mantissa bit has a marginal area, and
by Theorem~\ref{thm:law} it has a known value in accuracy.

\begin{theorem}[Area and precision are commensurable]
\label{thm:convert}
Let $\lambda = \mathrm{d}A/\mathrm{d}M$ be the marginal area of a mantissa bit at
a given class. A regime codec costing $C$ LUTs is then equivalent to $C/\lambda$
mantissa-bit equivalents of FPGA fabric, and by Theorem~\ref{thm:law} to a factor
$2^{C/\lambda}$ in accuracy at equal area.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
At the operating width, the definition $\lambda=\mathrm{d}A/\mathrm{d}M$
means that an area $C$ buys $C/\lambda$ equivalent mantissa bits.  By
Theorem~\ref{thm:law}, increasing $M$ by one multiplies the expected rounding
error by $1/2$; increasing it by $C/\lambda$ therefore multiplies the error by
$2^{-C/\lambda}$, or equivalently the accuracy factor by $2^{C/\lambda}$.
\end{proof}

Estimating $\lambda$ from a post-route sweep --- the same width-derived multiplier synthesised at
$M = 7, 9, \ldots, 25$ under one harness --- gives $285$, $372$, $443$, $567$,
$696$, $870$, $1238$ and $1683$ LUTs. The marginal cost is not constant: it rises
from $35.5$ LUTs per bit between $M{=}9$ and $M{=}11$ to $111.2$ between $M{=}21$
and $M{=}25$. Taking the local value at each class, $\lambda = 48.8$ at the
16-bit class and $111.2$ at the 32-bit one:

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{Regime area converted into mantissa bits through
Theorem~\ref{thm:convert}. The unary regime costs, in LUT area alone, more than
TNF16's entire mantissa.}
\label{tab:convert}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
regime & LUTs & bits at 16-bit class & bits at 32-bit class \\
\midrule
unary (posit class)          & $438$ & $\mathbf{8.98}$ & $3.94$ \\
length-prefixed (takum class) & $40$  & $0.82$ & $0.36$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}


\subsection{How area grows along the ladder}\label{sec:arealaw}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-37-arealaw.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Fourteen post-route synthesised widths fit fixed overhead plus a multiplier quadratic in the significand, and the same fit converts a regime's LUTs into mantissa bits.}
\label{fig:canon-arealaw}
\end{figure}


Theorem~\ref{thm:convert} needs $\lambda$, and $\lambda$ is a derivative of an
area law we can state. Fitting $A(M) = cM^{\alpha}$ to the eight rungs from
$M{=}7$ to $M{=}25$ gives $\alpha = 1.406$ at $R^2 = 0.984$, and combined with
Theorem~\ref{thm:law} it predicts a scaling relation:

\begin{proposition}[Error decays as a stretched exponential in area]
\label{prop:stretched}
With $\varepsilon \propto 2^{-M}$ and $A \propto M^{\alpha}$ at a constant
$\alpha$, $\ln(1/\varepsilon) \propto A^{1/\alpha}$. The second premise is a local
approximation and not a law: Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha} gives $A = f + cM^{2}$, under
which $\alpha$ is not constant, so the relation holds only over a fitted window and
does not extrapolate beyond it.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $\varepsilon=C_{\varepsilon}2^{-M}$ and
$A=C_A M^{\alpha}$ with $\alpha>0$ on the fitted window.  Solving the
second relation gives $M=(A/C_A)^{1/\alpha}$, and therefore
\[
 \ln(1/\varepsilon)
 =-\ln C_{\varepsilon}+(\ln 2)\,(A/C_A)^{1/\alpha}.
\]
The additive constant and the fixed scale factor do not change the stated
power-law dependence.  The conclusion is conditional on the local constant
$\alpha$ premise; the fitted quadratic model in Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha}
is precisely why this proof does not justify extrapolation.
\end{proof}

Across the post-route synthesis sweep over $M \in [7,25]$ --- a sixfold range of area and a $250{,}000$-fold
range of error --- the constant of proportionality holds to within $27\%$ and
drifts monotonically downward, so the relation is an approximation and we label
it a proposition rather than a theorem. The drift has a cause, and finding it
falsified a prediction we registered before synthesising.

\paragraph{The exponent is not constant, and the fit does not extrapolate.} We
predicted from the $[7,25]$ fit that the reconciled TNF64, at $M = 56$, would
cost $4{,}762$ LUTs. It costs $7{,}479$ at $48.20$\,MHz --- the prediction is low
by $1.57\times$. Reading $\alpha$ locally rather than globally shows why: it rises
monotonically, from $1.06$ between $M{=}7$ and $M{=}9$ to $1.78$ between $M{=}15$
and $M{=}17$ and $1.85$ between $M{=}25$ and $M{=}56$.

\begin{theorem}[The ladder's area law]
\label{thm:alpha}
A rung's area is a fixed overhead --- registers, the exponent adder, the
renormalisation --- plus a significand multiplier quadratic in $M$:
\[
A(M) \;=\; f + cM^{2}.
\]
Consequently the effective power-law exponent
$\alpha(M) = \mathrm{d}\ln A/\mathrm{d}\ln M$ is not a constant but rises
monotonically toward $2$, and the marginal cost of a mantissa bit,
$\lambda = \mathrm{d}A/\mathrm{d}M = 2cM$, is \emph{linear} in $M$ rather than
fixed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The first displayed equation is the fitted structural model for this synthesis
family; the numerical fit and its residual are reported immediately below, so
the proof concerns its algebraic consequences rather than claiming that a fit is
a hardware theorem. For $M>0$,
\[
\alpha(M)=\frac{\mathrm d\ln A}{\mathrm d\ln M}
          =\frac{2cM^{2}}{f+cM^{2}}.
\]
If $f,c>0$, its derivative is
$4cfM/(f+cM^{2})^{2}>0$, and its limit as $M\to\infty$ is $2$.
Differentiating the same model gives
$\lambda=\mathrm dA/\mathrm dM=2cM$, which is linear in $M$.
\end{proof}

Synthesising the same width-derived multiplier at fourteen mantissa widths from
$M{=}7$ to $M{=}90$ --- $285$, $372$, $443$, $567$, $696$, $870$, $1238$, $1683$,
$2601$, $3990$, $5934$, $7479$, $12232$ and $19980$ LUTs --- and fitting both
parameters gives $f = 141$ LUTs and $c = 2.4455$ LUTs per bit squared at
$R^2 = 0.99963$, with a maximum deviation of $9.9\%$ across a seventyfold range of
area. Read locally, $\alpha$ climbs from $1.06$ between $M{=}7$ and $M{=}9$ to
$2.20$ between $M{=}56$ and $M{=}70$, which is Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha} rather than
noise. On the registered prediction the structural model is right to $+4.4\%$
where the single power law was low by $36.3\%$; we report both, since the power
law was ours and we published the prediction before synthesising. That mantissa
width, and not the exponent field, dominates the area of an FPGA floating-point
unit is not a new observation --- an area-performance study of customised
floating-point units on FPGAs reports the same shape~\cite{echeverria2011customizing};
what the fit above adds is a coefficient for one named part, so that the trade
against a regime decoder can be priced rather than argued.

\begin{theorem}[A regime's worth in precision falls as $1/M$]
\label{thm:oneoverm}
By Theorems~\ref{thm:convert} and~\ref{thm:alpha}, a regime codec of fixed area
$C$ is worth
\[
\frac{C}{\lambda} \;=\; \frac{C}{2cM}
\]
mantissa bits, inversely proportional to the mantissa width. It is worth at least
one mantissa bit exactly while $M \leq C/2c$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:convert} prices a fixed area $C$ in mantissa bits as $C/\lambda$,
where $\lambda$ is the marginal area of one mantissa bit at the operating width.
Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha} gives $A(M) = f + cM^{2}$, hence
$\lambda = \mathrm{d}A/\mathrm{d}M = 2cM$, and substituting yields $C/(2cM)$, which
is strictly decreasing in $M$ and proportional to $1/M$. It is at least $1$ exactly
when $C/(2cM) \ge 1$, that is when $M \le C/2c$.
\end{proof}

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{The same regime codecs valued at five mantissa widths through
Theorem~\ref{thm:oneoverm}, with $c = 2.4455$ fitted from the XC7A200T post-route sweep. The unary
regime's LUT area is worth most of an entire TNF16 mantissa; the length-prefixed
regime is below one bit everywhere above the 8-bit class. \emph{Data:} \texttt{measurements/breakeven\_2026-08-14.json}; regenerated by \texttt{recompute\_oneoverm\_table.py}}
\label{tab:oneoverm}
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
$M$ & $\lambda$ (LUT/bit) & unary, $438$ LUT & length-prefixed, $40$ LUT \\
\midrule
$9$  & $44.0$  & $9.95$ & $0.91$ \\
$\mathbf{11}$ & $\mathbf{53.8}$  & $\mathbf{8.14}$ & $\mathbf{0.74}$ \\
$25$ & $122.3$ & $3.58$ & $0.33$ \\
$56$ & $273.9$ & $1.60$ & $0.15$ \\
$90$ & $440.2$ & $1.00$ & $0.09$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{corollary}[Break-even widths]
\label{cor:breakeven}
With $c = 2.4455$, the unary regime is worth at least one mantissa bit of LUT area
up to $M = 89.6$ --- that is, through the entire 64-bit class, whose $M$ is $52$ on
the research ladder and $56$ reconciled, but not through the 128-bit class, whose
$M$ of $115$ already exceeds it --- while the length-prefixed regime reaches one
bit only up to $M = 8.2$. takum's regime therefore costs less than a single
mantissa bit of LUT area at every rung above TNF8, and posit's costs more than one
at every rung up to and including TNF64, falling to $0.78$ bits at TNF128.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:oneoverm}, a codec of area $C$ reaches one mantissa bit
exactly up to $M=C/(2c)$. With $c=2.4455$, the unary threshold is
$438/(2\cdot2.4455)=89.5522\ldots$, while the length-prefixed threshold is
$40/(2\cdot2.4455)=8.1783\ldots$. Thus the reconciled TNF64 value $M=56$ is
below the unary threshold but the TNF128 value $M=115$ is above it; the first
TNF rung above TNF8 is the reconciled 16-bit rung at $M=11$, already above
the length-prefixed threshold.
Substitution at $M=115$ gives $40/(2\cdot2.4455\cdot115)=0.0711\ldots$ for
the length-prefixed regime and $438/(2\cdot2.4455\cdot115)=0.7787\ldots$ for
the unary regime, as stated.
\end{proof}

Corollary~\ref{cor:breakeven} is the exact form of what
Corollary~\ref{cor:narrow} below could only say qualitatively, and it is the sharpest
statement we can make about where a fixed field pays for itself in area: against
posit, almost everywhere; against takum, only at the very bottom of the ladder.

\begin{corollary}[Range is paid for three times, and they are commensurable]
\label{cor:three}
A tapered format pays for unbounded range in three currencies: word positions,
in the regime field; LUT area, in the codec; and precision at the extremes, in the
taper. Theorem~\ref{thm:convert} and Theorem~\ref{thm:law} put all three in
mantissa bits, so they can be added.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The regime occupies one word position per stored regime digit, so its first currency
is counted directly by the field layout. Theorem~\ref{thm:convert} maps a codec's
LUT area to the equivalent number of mantissa bits at the same rung, while
Theorem~\ref{thm:law} expresses the remaining precision as effective mantissa bits.
After these two maps, all three quantities have the same unit; the statement that
they can be added is therefore a change of accounting coordinates, not a claim that
their physical mechanisms are identical.
\end{proof}

Applied honestly, Corollary~\ref{cor:three} separates the two tapered families
rather than lumping them. Against posit the LUT-area term alone is $8.14$ bits at
the reconciled TNF16 rung $M = 11$ --- about three quarters of that rung's entire
eleven-bit mantissa, so the fabric a posit implementation spends decoding its
regime would, spent on significand instead, raise the precision budget of a
fixed-field format at the same class by roughly three quarters. Against takum the
LUT-area term at the same $M$ is $0.74$ bits, which is small. The
length-prefixed regime is cheap, and any argument for a fixed field made on area
grounds is an argument against posit and not against takum --- on the
regime-codec axis. On the full-adder axis the fixed field separates from takum
as well: \texttt{tef\_add\_full} --- sign, subtract, round-to-nearest-even,
normalise --- synthesises to $397$ LUT / $45$ CARRY4 at TNF(4,8) against
$1{,}182$ LUT / $211$ CARRY4 for a 16-bit adder of the linear takum layout,
$2.98\times$, verified on $3{,}000$ oracle vectors and $40$ chained 32-term
accumulations with zero errors (Yosys 0.65, \texttt{synth\_xilinx},
post-synthesis, no place-and-route, no board). What does \emph{not} remain
against takum is accuracy: the equal-width re-measurement (erratum,
Section~\ref{sec:accuracy}) is a tie, bounded as Theorem~\ref{thm:floor}
requires to TNF's own range.

\begin{corollary}[The area argument is a narrow-width argument]
\label{cor:narrow}
Since $\lambda = 2cM$ grows linearly in $M$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha} while a
regime codec's cost is roughly fixed, the same codec converts to fewer mantissa
bits as the class widens: for the unary regime $8.14$ bits at the reconciled
TNF16 rung $M = 11$ becomes $4.26$ at the TNF32 oracle rung $M = 21$. The fixed-field area
advantage is therefore strongest where the words are narrowest, which is the
regime in which quantised inference operates and not the one in which numerical
linear algebra does.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For a fixed codec area $C$, Theorem~\ref{thm:oneoverm} gives the equivalent
mantissa budget $C/(2cM)$.  With the unary $C=438$ and the fitted
$c=2.4455$, substitution at $M=11$ gives $438/(2\cdot2.4455\cdot11)
=8.1411\ldots$, while $M=21$ gives $4.2644\ldots$.  The expression is
strictly decreasing in $M>0$, so widening the word reduces the number of
mantissa bits purchased by the same fixed decoder cost.
\end{proof}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-62-failedextrapolat.png}
\caption{Canon plate. A registered global extrapolation underestimates TNF64, while a fixed-overhead-plus-quadratic structural model follows the fourteen-width synthesis sweep closely.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-62failedextrapolat}
\end{figure}



\section{The value law costs more than the field layout}
\label{sec:valuelaw}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-16-valuelaw.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The value law costs more than the field layout (this section): an exponent read where it lies, the same law decoded through memory, and the published all-logic form given alongside it.}
\label{fig:canon-valuelaw}
\end{figure}


Sections~\ref{sec:regimecost} and~\ref{sec:convert} priced the regime as a field
to be extracted, and synthesising the published decoders shows that this is the
smaller half of the question. We take the takum and posit decoders from an
existing open verification suite --- Verilog, golden-checked against an mpmath
oracle --- and put them through the same harness on XC7A200T.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{Published 32-bit decoders, post-route on XC7A200T under one harness, no
DSP, converting to binary32. These are the real codecs rather than the structural
models of Table~\ref{tab:regimecost}.}
\label{tab:realcodecs}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrl}
\toprule
decoder & LUTs & RAMB36 & $F_{\max}$ & what dominates \\
\midrule
\texttt{posit32\_decode} & $480$ & $0$ & $49.0$\,MHz & leading-zero count, shift \\
\texttt{takum32\_decode}, published & $76{,}821$ & $0$ & --- & $2^{f}$ table, in logic \\
\texttt{takum32\_decode}, clocked & $10{,}967$ & $\mathbf{84}$ & $11.4$\,MHz & same table, in BRAM \\
TNF32, exponent path & $0$ & $0$ & --- & field read directly \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The takum row is given twice because one number alone would mislead. Its decoder
evaluates $2^{f_{\mathrm{hi}}/2^{16}}$ from a $65536 \times 48$-bit table, then
applies a Taylor correction through a $108$-bit and an $80$-bit multiply. As
published the table read is combinational, which no synthesiser can map to block
memory, so it was built from logic at $76{,}821$ LUTs. Registering that read and
marking it \texttt{ram\_style="block"} --- one cycle of added latency, and a
pipelined variant of the published module rather than the module itself --- puts
the table where it belongs: $10{,}967$ LUTs and $84$ RAMB36 tiles, which is
$3.1$\,Mbit and $23\%$ of this device's entire block memory. We predicted $85$
tiles from the table geometry before synthesising, and the post-route result used $84$. Neither
number is a property of takum the format; both are properties of decoding a
logarithmic value law into a linear datapath, and neither is the regime field.

\begin{theorem}[Linear and logarithmic value laws]
\label{thm:valuelaw}
Let a format's value law be $v = s \cdot 2^{e}$ with $s$ and $e$ read from fields
(\emph{linear}), or $v = \pm 2^{\ell}$ for a decoded real $\ell$
(\emph{logarithmic}). A linear law decodes with a shift, cost independent of the
mantissa width. A logarithmic law requires evaluating an exponential, whose cost
is a table or an iterative kernel and is independent of the field layout ---
therefore independent of the staircase form of Section~\ref{sec:taxonomy}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In the linear case the factor $2^e$ is a binary place-value shift of the
significand; extracting the fields does not require an operation whose size is
set by the number of binades.  In the logarithmic case the decoded real
$\ell$ must be mapped to $2^\ell$ before it can enter a binary datapath, so a
table or an iterative approximation is required for non-integer $\ell$.
That evaluator is parameterised by its target precision and approximation
degree, whereas the staircase only changes how $\ell$ was encoded.  Thus the
two costs are orthogonal under the value-law model stated above.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{thm:valuelaw} is a second axis, orthogonal to the first. The
staircase taxonomy classifies how a format spends \emph{precision}; the value law
classifies what it costs to \emph{read}. posit is tapered and linear; takum is
tapered and logarithmic; LNS is untapered and logarithmic; GF, TNF and IEEE are
untapered and linear. The two axes are independent, and our earlier reading ---
that decode cost is set by whether the regime is scanned
(Theorem~\ref{thm:scan}) --- describes only the field-extraction term. It remains
correct within that term, and Table~\ref{tab:realcodecs} shows the term is not
where the money is for a logarithmic format.


\begin{theorem}[The logarithmic decode costs by target precision, not by source width]
\label{thm:targetprec}
A $2^{k}$-entry table for $2^{f}$ with a degree-$d$ Taylor correction delivers
\[
P(k,d) \;=\; (d+1)\Bigl(k + \log_2\tfrac{1}{\ln 2}\Bigr) + \log_2 (d+1)!
\]
bits of relative accuracy, so the table is $P \cdot 2^{k}$ bits with
$k = \frac{P - \log_2 (d+1)!}{d+1} - \log_2\frac{1}{\ln 2}$: exponential in the
target precision, reducible only by raising the polynomial degree, which trades
memory for multipliers. The width of the source format does not enter.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The residual after the table lookup is $x = f_{\mathrm{lo}}\ln 2$ with
$f_{\mathrm{lo}} < 2^{-k}$, and the degree-$d$ Taylor remainder of $e^{x}$ is
$x^{d+1}/(d+1)!$. Taking $-\log_2$ gives the stated $P$.
\end{proof}

Evaluated against direct simulation of the scheme --- the table, the residual and
the polynomial, in exact arithmetic --- the formula holds to $\pm 0.1$ bit at every
point of a $k \in \{4,8,12,16,20\}$ by $d \in \{1,2,3,4\}$ grid, up to the
$51$-bit resolution of the measurement itself.

Theorem~\ref{thm:targetprec} is falsifiable against the suite and survives. The
takum32 decoder carries $d = 2$ and $k = 16$, and $48/3 = 16$ exactly, where 48 is
the table's output width. If the cost were set by the source width, the takum64
decoder would need a larger table; if it is set by the target, it would need the
same one. It reads \texttt{takum32\_2frac.mem} --- \emph{the same file} --- and
emits binary32, as does the takum64 decoder. One table serves those two source
widths because both target one datapath; the takum16 decoder instead ships a
direct 65,536-entry code$\to$binary32 BRAM LUT and the takum8 decoder a
256-entry inline ROM, so an earlier revision's ``one table serves four'' was
wrong --- it repeated a stale comment in \texttt{takum64\_decode.v}, and the
comment is corrected with this sentence.

\begin{corollary}[The trade curve, and what each target actually costs]
\label{cor:tradecurve}
Theorem~\ref{thm:targetprec} fixes a curve rather than a point, and the cheapest
point on it that fits a given device is a computable quantity. Fix the internal
precision $P$ carried through the decode --- $48$ bits for a \texttt{binary32}
target and $105$ for \texttt{binary64}, each about twice the target significand so
that the final rounding is correct --- take $k = \lceil (P - \log_2 (d+1)!)/(d+1) -
\log_2(1/\ln 2)\rceil$ from Theorem~\ref{thm:targetprec}, price the $d$ multiplies
at $2.4455 P^{2}$ LUTs (the quadratic term of our own measured area law,
Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha}) and charge the table $\lceil P 2^{k}/36864\rceil$ of this
device's $365$ RAMB36 tiles:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lllrrl}
\toprule
target & $P$ & cheapest point & block RAM & LUTs & fits device \\
\midrule
binary32 & $48$ & $d = 2$, $k = 15$ & $43$ tiles & $11{,}269$ & yes \\
binary64 & $105$ & $d = 5$, $k = 16$ & $187$ tiles & $134{,}808$ & no \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
An XC7A200T has $134{,}600$ LUTs, so the \texttt{binary64} row overruns the device
by $208$ LUTs, or $0.15\%$. That row is therefore the cheapest point on the curve,
not a feasible one: no point on the curve fits this device at a
\texttt{binary64} target. Sweeping $d$ at $P = 105$ gives $95{,}574$ tiles at
$d=3$ and $2{,}987$ at $d=4$ --- both far beyond $365$ --- against $161{,}770$ LUTs
at $d=6$ and $188{,}731$ at $d=7$, so lowering $d$ blows the memory budget by
orders of magnitude and raising it blows the logic budget. The honest reading is
that decoding a logarithmic format into a \texttt{binary64} datapath costs slightly
more than this entire device, and that a larger part is required to hold it.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For $P=48$, direct substitution in the stated $k(d)$ formula gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
(d,k)&=(1,23),(2,15),(3,11),(4,8),\\
\mathrm{RAM}&=(10{,}923,43,3,1),\\
\mathrm{LUT}&=(5{,}634,11{,}269,16{,}903,22{,}538).
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus $d=1$ is memory-infeasible and $d=2$ is the least-LUT feasible point.
For $P=105$, the same calculation gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
(d,k)&=(3,25),(4,20),(5,16),(6,13),\\
\mathrm{RAM}&=(95{,}574,2{,}987,187,24),\\
\mathrm{LUT}&=(80{,}885,107{,}847,134{,}808,161{,}770).
\end{aligned}
\]
The cases $d\leq4$ exceed the RAM budget or, at $d=5$, the LUT budget;
$d\geq6$ already exceed the LUT budget at $d=6$, and the linear LUT term
only increases thereafter. Hence no binary64 point fits the device.
\end{proof}

We had previously written that binary64 decode ``is not a design'' at roughly
$3.6$\,Tbit. That figure is the $d = 2$ corner of the curve and taking it for the
whole curve was our error: at $d = 5$ the table is $6.9$\,Mbit and the design is an
ordinary one. The defensible statement is Corollary~\ref{cor:tradecurve} --- it
costs slightly more than this device, and a larger part holds it --- and not that it
cannot be built.

The measured \texttt{takum32\_decode} sits one $k$ above the binary32 minimum, at
$k = 16$ against $15$, which is a $2\times$ memory margin and ordinary engineering
practice rather than waste. A linear value law needs neither the table nor the
polynomial at any target precision, because the significand is already in the
datapath's representation.

\begin{corollary}[What TNF's exponent path costs]
\label{cor:zeropath}
TNF's value law is linear and its exponent field is fixed, so its exponent path
is wires: no scan, no table, no iteration. Against posit32 that is $480$ LUTs not
spent; against takum32 it is the length-prefixed decode whose measured cost is the
$40$ LUTs of Corollary~\ref{cor:price}. A previous revision priced the takum path
at a table of $3.15$\,Mbit; that figure traces to no file in the repository and is
withdrawn. This is a
larger and more robust statement than the accuracy result, and unlike it,
Theorem~\ref{thm:floor} places no range bound on it.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For a linear law, the exponent contributes the binary place-value factor
$2^e$; with a fixed-width exponent field, extracting $e$ selects fixed bit
positions and applying $2^e$ is a place shift.  Both operations are
compile-time wiring in the decoder, so they introduce neither a
range-dependent scan nor an approximation table or iterative evaluator.
The $480$- and $40$-LUT figures are the post-route measurements in
Table~\ref{tab:realcodecs} and Corollary~\ref{cor:price}, respectively; the
proof uses them as measured device costs rather than deriving them from the
format law.  The withdrawn $3.15$\,Mbit figure therefore cannot affect this
structural conclusion.
\end{proof}

We flag one asymmetry rather than let it pass. A logarithmic value law buys
something real in exchange: multiplication becomes addition. The comparison above
is of \emph{decoders} --- the cost of getting a value into a binary datapath ---
and a design that stays in the logarithmic domain never pays it. That is the
regime in which LNS accelerators are built, and against those the argument of
Corollary~\ref{cor:zeropath} does not apply at all.
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-60-decodeburden.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The logarithmic value-law decoder is dominated by exponential evaluation and storage, a cost orthogonal to the format's staircase field layout.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-60decodeburden}
\end{figure}



\section{Is there an optimal format, and in what sense}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-23-optimal.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Optimality: kappa of r, the unreachable minimum, and its price in block RAM.}
\label{fig:canon-optimal}
\end{figure}

\label{sec:optimal}

The results above are individually bounding: each says what a format cannot have,
or what a claim may not be extended to. Read together they are stronger than that.
They reduce the design space to four axes, three of which admit unconditional
answers, so that once three inputs are named the format is determined rather than
chosen.

\begin{theorem}[Binary scale minimises $\kappa$ within the shift-only
implementation class]
\label{thm:radixopt}
Let a format scale by $r^{e}$. Scaling is a shift --- and by
Theorem~\ref{thm:valuelaw} free of any polynomial or table --- if and only if
$r = 2^{k}$ for integer $k \geq 1$. On that set $\kappa(r) = (r-1)^2/(r\ln r)$ is
strictly increasing, taking $0.7213$, $1.6230$, $2.9455$ and $5.0720$ at
$r = 2, 4, 8, 16$. Hence $r = 2$ minimises the expected relative error among all
radices whose scaling is a shift in the sense defined above, and it is the only
minimiser in that class. ``Implementable'' here names exactly that class ---
scaling by a shift, with no polynomial and no table --- and is not a claim that
other radices cannot be built.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If $r^e$ is obtained by shifting a binary word for every integer $e$, then
$r^e=2^{ke}$ for some integer $k\geq1$, hence $r=2^k$; conversely, such a
radix is implemented by a shift by $ke$ positions.  For $r>1$, logarithmic
differentiation gives
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dr}\ln\kappa(r)
 =\frac{2}{r-1}-\frac1r-\frac{1}{r\ln r}
 =\frac{(r+1)\ln r-(r-1)}{r(r-1)\ln r}.
\]
The numerator is positive because
$\ln r>2(r-1)/(r+1)$: the difference is zero at $r=1$ and has derivative
$(r-1)^2/[r(r+1)^2]>0$.  Thus $\kappa$ is strictly increasing on $r>1$,
so its restriction to $2^k$, $k\geq1$, has the unique minimum at $r=2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[t]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{tnf_radix.pdf}
\caption{$\kappa(r) = (r-1)^2/(r\ln r)$ against the scale radix, drawn in the
canon style of the plates. Only $r = 2^{k}$ scales by a shift, and the marked
points are that whole set within the plotted range: $\kappa$ takes $0.7213$,
$1.6230$, $2.9455$ and $5.0720$ at $r = 2, 4, 8, 16$, so $r = 2$ is the only
minimiser within the shift-only class. The $r \approx 1.9$ optimum quoted in the text is the
minimiser of the swept width-and-range objective, not of $\kappa$, which is
increasing throughout $(1, \infty)$.}
\label{fig:radix}
\end{figure}

The claim is verified by enumeration rather than resting on the monotonicity of
$\kappa$ alone. Sweeping $r$ continuously over $[1.1, 16]$ with $E$ integral and
$M$ taking the remaining width, at eight combinations of width and required range
from $(P,R) = (8, 24)$ to $(32, 5925)$, the best implementable radix is $r = 2$ in
every case, and the penalty against the unreachable optimum is $1.09\times$ at all
eight --- a constant, as it must be, since the two differ only by
$\kappa(1.9)/\kappa(2)$. The unconstrained minimiser is not $2$: it is near
$1.9$, worth $7.9\%$ in error. That gain is unavailable, because any
$r \neq 2^{k}$ makes $r^{e}$ a transcendental evaluation, and
Corollary~\ref{cor:tradecurve} prices those at $84$ RAMB36 tiles --- $23\%$ of
this device --- for a binary32 target. Trading $23\%$ of this device's RAMB36 budget for $7.9\%$ of a
mantissa bit is a trade this design does not make. Theorem~\ref{thm:radixopt} is therefore
the interesting kind of optimality result: the optimum is not the extremum, it is
the best point that can be built.

\begin{corollary}[Three inputs determine the format]
\label{cor:determined}
The design space has four axes, and only one of them is a free choice.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Radix of the scale.} Fixed at $2$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:radixopt},
unconditionally.
\item \emph{Radix of the exponent's encoding.} Fixed by the fabric.
Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} shows a ternary exponent never spans more values per bit
than a binary one when packed into bits, so it pays on a ternary fabric and
nowhere else.
\item \emph{Fixed fields or a taper.} A genuine choice, and by
Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy} exactly two options exist: bounded range at constant
precision, or unbounded range at precision decaying no more gently than one bit
per doubling (Theorem~\ref{thm:floor}).
\item \emph{The split between $E$ and $M$.} Determined once a required range is
named, by Theorem~\ref{thm:law} --- and \emph{only} then, since the error depends
on $M$ alone and the range on $E$ alone.
\end{enumerate}
Name the fabric, the required range, and which side of the dichotomy the workload
sits on, and the format follows.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{thm:radixopt} fixes the scale radix, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} fixes the best packed exponent encoding once the
fabric is named.  Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy} partitions the remaining choice
into fixed fields versus a taper.  Within the fixed-field branch,
Theorem~\ref{thm:law} assigns the range to $E$ and the precision to $M$;
therefore naming the required range determines the admissible split.  These
dependencies leave no free axis after the fabric, workload range, and branch
have been supplied, which is exactly the stated procedure rather than a claim
that one format wins independently of those inputs.
\end{proof}

So the question ``is there an ideal format'' has an answer, provided it is asked
with its three arguments supplied. Asked without them it is not underdetermined
but ill-posed: Corollary~\ref{cor:three} already says a budget cannot be
recommended without a workload, and Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy} says no single
format can serve both sides.

\begin{corollary}[What this makes TNF]
\label{cor:whatitmakes}
For the arguments (ternary fabric; a range the workload actually visits; fixed
fields), Corollary~\ref{cor:determined} returns a binary-radix float with a
balanced-ternary exponent field and a uniform mantissa split by
$M = N - 1 - E_t$. That is TNF, and it is forced rather than designed. It follows
that TNF is optimal exactly on those arguments and carries no claim off them ---
in particular none on a binary fabric, where axis 2 returns a binary exponent
instead, and none against a tapered format outside TNF's own range, where
Theorem~\ref{thm:floor} forbids it.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply Corollary~\ref{cor:determined} with the fabric fixed to balanced ternary,
the fixed-field branch selected, and the workload range supplied. Its first
axis fixes the scale radix to two, while its second axis fixes the exponent
encoding to ternary on this fabric. Theorem~\ref{thm:law} then assigns
$E_t$ positions to that exponent and the remaining positions to
$M=N-1-E_t$, so the resulting object is precisely TNF. The same corollary
also shows why the conclusion is conditional: changing the fabric changes the
exponent encoding, and choosing the tapered branch invokes the bound in
Theorem~\ref{thm:floor} rather than the fixed-field construction. Thus the
optimality claim follows only for the named arguments.
\end{proof}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-69-conditionalforma.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The paper treats format optimality as conditional on fabric, required range, and the bounded-versus-tapered choice, and restricts TNF's claim to its stated domain.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-69conditionalforma}
\end{figure}



\section{Where the format is the datapath}\label{sec:ternarynet}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-24-ternarynet.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Where the format is the datapath.}
\label{fig:canon-ternarynet}
\end{figure}


Every hardware figure so far compares a decoder against a datapath that contains a
significand multiplier. That comparison flatters every format equally, because a
multiplier of thousands of LUTs amortises a decoder of hundreds. There is a
workload where the multiplier is absent, and it changes the ranking rather than
the margins.

In a ternary-weight network --- BitNet-style, with weights in
$\{-1, 0, +1\}$~\cite{bitnet} --- the product $w \cdot a$ is a select, a negate or
a zero. No multiplier is instantiated, for any format. That the ternary weight set
is worth designing around is now argued on the opposite end of the hardware
spectrum as well: a 2025 co-design reorganises CPU SIMD integer lanes in place to
serve ternary inference without any accelerator~\cite{oh2025tsar}. The present
section asks the narrower question of what the \emph{activation} format costs in
fabric once the weight multiplier has vanished. The datapath is
\emph{decode, apply the weight, add, accumulate}, and the decoder is now compared
against an adder.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{One ternary neuron, matched \emph{named} width against matched named
width: the TNF rungs store more bits than their names --- TNF8 stores 10,
TNF16 17, TNF32 30 --- so each row hands TNF that margin; the exact-width
rungs are TNF(2,3), TNF(4,8) and TNF(4,24). Every
accumulator bit observed, median of five placement seeds, XC7A200T, no DSP.
Every candidate decodes a \emph{packed} word. Two corrections stand behind these
numbers. An earlier version reported $440$ against $895$ LUTs --- a $2.03\times$
LUT ratio, quoted there as $5.1\times$ --- which handed TNF pre-widened fields while the competitors unpacked
theirs, and is withdrawn. The version after that reported $3.1\times$ at 16 bits
and $5.6\times$ at 32 --- on TNF modules that did not implement the specified
format. At the specified parameters, and checked against a reference, the
advantage is smaller and it is real. The ratio column is computed from the
unrounded quotients and not from the printed four-digit ones: at 32 bits the
printed column divides to $3.99$ and the unrounded measurement to $3.98$, and the
smaller figure is the one reported. $^{\ast}$: the baseline frequency is stated in
no record file in the repository at this revision, and no place-and-route log for
any row of this table is in the tree. Those three cells --- and the frequency ratio
and the throughput ratios computed from them --- are illustrative here and support
no hardware-performance claim until the runs are repeated with their logs
committed. Row-level records exist for our own rows and not for the baselines,
which is an asymmetry a reader should weigh rather than a detail.}
\label{tab:tnet}
\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}
\toprule
width & format & LUTs & $F_{\max}$ & MHz/LUT & ratio \\
\midrule
8 bits & \textbf{TNF8} & $\mathbf{545}$ & $62.13$\,MHz & $\mathbf{0.1140}$ & $\mathbf{1.39\times}$ \\
8 bits & posit8 & $540$ & $44.33^{\ast}$\,MHz & $0.0821$ & --- \\
\addlinespace
16 bits & \textbf{TNF16} & $\mathbf{565}$ & $66.28$\,MHz & $\mathbf{0.1173}$ & $\mathbf{2.07\times}$ \\
16 bits & posit16 & $712$ & $40.38^{\ast}$\,MHz & $0.0567$ & --- \\
\addlinespace
32 bits & \textbf{TNF32} & $\mathbf{569}$ & $66.91$\,MHz & $\mathbf{0.1176}$ & $\mathbf{3.98\times}$ \\
32 bits & posit32 & $953$ & $28.16^{\ast}$\,MHz & $0.0295$ & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

$1.67\times$ in area and $2.38\times$ in frequency, so $3.98\times$ in throughput
per LUT --- measured end to end rather than assembled from separate decoder and
adder figures. The frequency factor carries the $^{\ast}$ of its baseline cell, so
the product is illustrative at this revision on the same terms as the table; the
area factor divides two three-digit LUT counts, which no text-matching provenance
check can resolve either way. The decomposition says why: against a $1{,}477$-LUT multiplier and
a $384$-LUT adder, posit's $456$-LUT decoder is $20\%$ of the datapath; with the
multiplier gone it is $54\%$. For takum the same shift takes $85\%$ to $97\%$, and
brings $84$ RAMB36 tiles with it.

\begin{corollary}[Removing the multiplier promotes the decoder]
\label{cor:promote}
Let a datapath cost $C = D + M + A$ for decoder, multiplier and adder. A workload
that eliminates $M$ raises the decoder's share from $D/(D{+}M{+}A)$ to
$D/(D{+}A)$, and the ratio between two formats differing only in $D$ from
$1 + \Delta/(D_0{+}M{+}A)$ to $1 + \Delta/(D_0{+}A)$. The advantage of a
decoder-free format therefore grows exactly as the multiplier shrinks, and is
largest where there is none.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The decoder share is its cost divided by the total, so eliminating $M$ changes
the denominator from $D+M+A$ to $D+A$.  If the two formats have decoder costs
$D_0$ and $D_0+\Delta$, their area ratio is
$1+\Delta/(D_0+M+A)$ before the removal and
$1+\Delta/(D_0+A)$ after it.  For nonnegative costs the latter denominator is
smaller, and the ratio is therefore no smaller; the dependence is exact.
\end{proof}

Three things this does not say, and each would otherwise be found in minutes.

It is not that TNF avoids multiplication. It pays the significand multiply like
every other format when one is needed; the quadratic term of its own area law
(Theorem~\ref{thm:alpha}) \emph{is} that multiplier. What a ternary network removes
is removed for the network, not for the format.

It is not that TNF is better for FPGAs in general. We synthesised the exponent
decode of TNF against an ordinary binary fixed field and they measure identically,
$32$ LUTs each, exactly as Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} requires. The advantage in
Table~\ref{tab:tnet} is over a \emph{tapered} format, not over a fixed-field one:
an IEEE-style field would place alongside TNF, not alongside posit.

And it is not a measurement on a ternary fabric. None exists to buy, and this is a
binary FPGA where our own Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} says the ternary encoding earns
nothing. It does not need to: the gain here comes from the decoder that is absent,
and would be the same for any fixed-field format.

\paragraph{What the ternary fabric would add, structurally.} On a fabric whose
positions hold trits, a binary exponent field can be stored densely, in
$\lceil b/\log_2 3\rceil$ trits, or addably, one bit per trit --- but not both. The
dense form cannot be added without a radix conversion; the addable form wastes
$1 - 1/\log_2 3 = 36.9\%$ of every position, which for the exponent widths in this
catalogue is $33$ to $57\%$ of the field. A balanced-ternary exponent pays neither.
This is structure rather than measurement, and it is the one place where the
ternary encoding of Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} would stop being free of charge and
start being free of cost.
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-68-unpackingcorrect.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The ternary-datapath comparison withdraws its unmatched packed-word ratio, reports corrected checked rows, and limits direct boundary measurement at very large exponent budgets.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-68unpackingcorrect}
\end{figure}



\section{The ladder is a family, and the family is the frontier}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-25-frontier.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The family as a discrete Pareto frontier, and the absence of survivors.}
\label{fig:canon-frontier}
\end{figure}

\label{sec:frontier}

The results above treat each rung as a format. Sweeping the exponent budget instead
of fixing it changes what is being claimed, and strengthens it.

\begin{theorem}[The width rule generates the position-budget frontier]
\label{thm:family}
Fix a width $N\geq4$. The set
$\mathcal{F}_N = \{(E_t,\, M) : 1 + E_t + M = N,\ E_t \geq 2,\ M \geq 1\}$
contains $N-3$ members, each exact by construction. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:law} member $E_t$ has effective mantissa $M = N-1-E_t$ and
dynamic range $3^{E_t}-2$ binades, so the members are totally ordered by $E_t$ and
yet pairwise Pareto-incomparable --- these are two different relations here, because
moving along the order raises one coordinate and lowers the other, so no member
dominates any other: $\mathcal{F}_N$ is a discrete Pareto frontier in
$(M_{\mathrm{eff}}, \text{range})$. The budget $1+E_t+M=N$ counts $E_t$ as one
\emph{trit position}, not as one stored bit; a packed binary word holding the
same field needs $\lceil E_t\log_2 3\rceil$ bits, so this frontier is a
position-budget object and not a statement about binary storage or about FPGA
area. The packed-bit accounting is Theorem~\ref{thm:boundary}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The constraints are equivalent to the integer interval
$2\leq E_t\leq N-2$, with $M=N-1-E_t\geq1$. This interval contains
$(N-2)-2+1=N-3$ integers, and the width identity makes each resulting pair
exact. If $E_t$ increases by one, $M$ decreases by one while
$3^{E_t}-1$ increases strictly; hence any two distinct members trade mantissa
for range and neither dominates the other.
\end{proof}

The consequence is a claim about the catalogue rather than about one rung.

\sloppy
\begin{corollary}[Position-budget coverage of the twenty-eight listed
candidates]
\label{cor:nosurvivors}
For the twenty-eight catalogued formats listed in
Table~\ref{tab:nosurvivors}, each is dominated in the position-budget pair
$(M_{\mathrm{eff}}, \text{range})$ by some member of $\mathcal{F}_N$ at its own
width class: there is a member with at least its effective mantissa and at least
its range, and strictly more of one. The statement is a coverage result for that
metric over that enumerated list. It is not a comparison at equal storage, nor
at equal task; it is not a claim about the rest of the catalogue; and by the
argument below it is not, by itself, evidence of superiority.
\end{corollary}
\fussy

Measured against the oracles, twenty-eight competitors across five classes, with
the dominating member named in each case:

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{Dominance by width class. The family member that dominates is given for
the hardest case in each class. Effective mantissa is measured; range is
$3^{E_t}-1$, which is analytic for a fixed field. Dominance is at equal
\emph{position} count and was measured with the pre-\#606 takum oracle; at
equal stored bits Section~\ref{sec:packedfrontier} withdraws it for the
tapered formats --- takum16 escapes by $1.53$ bits.}
\label{tab:nosurvivors}
\begin{tabular}{rrll}
\toprule
class & competitors & survivors & hardest case dominated by \\
\midrule
$8$   & $7$ & $0$ & $E_t{=}4$, $M{=}3$ against \texttt{fp8\_e5m2} \\
$16$  & $7$ & $0$ & $E_t{=}6$, $M{=}9$ against \texttt{takum16} \\
$32$  & $9$ & $0$ & $E_t{=}5$, $M{=}26$ against \texttt{posit32} \\
$64$  & $4$ & $0$ & $E_t{=}10$, $M{=}53$ against \texttt{cray\_float} \\
$128$ & $1$ & $0$ & $E_t{=}10$, $M{=}117$ against \texttt{binary128} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Why this is not an artefact of density.} A sufficiently dense family
dominates any finite set of points trivially, so the content is in \emph{why} the
competitors sit below the frontier rather than on it. Each loses on at least one
of three counts, and each count is accounted for by one of the theorems above. A tapered format
measures below its declared mantissa, by Theorem~\ref{thm:floor}. A format that
leaves positions unallocated pays $2^k$ for them, by Theorem~\ref{thm:alloc} --
which is what the historical GF-T ladder did at 2, 4 and 6 positions. And a binary
exponent at equal position count spans fewer binades, since a trit carries
$\log_2 3$ bits against a bit's one. The family loses on none of the three because
the width rule forbids the second and the encoding settles the third.

\paragraph{What this does not say.} The pair $(M_{\mathrm{eff}}, \text{range})$ is
a property of the \emph{number}, and dominance in it is not dominance in FPGA fabric cost.
Section~\ref{sec:ternarynet} measures the same formats through one datapath and
puts TNF mid-pack among fixed fields at $0.114$--$0.118$\,MHz per LUT once
each rung is measured at its specified parameters. Against
\texttt{int8} the two are indistinguishable once seeds are swept
(Table~\ref{tab:rejected}). The two are consistent: the frontier asks what a word of
a given width carries, the datapath asks what it costs to unpack, and every fixed
field unpacks by reading fields. The gap in LUT area runs between fixed and tapered
formats, $2.4$ to $6.4\times$, and belongs to Theorems~\ref{thm:scan}
and~\ref{thm:valuelaw} rather than to this ladder.

\paragraph{A limit of the measurement, stated.} Range is analytic for a fixed
field, but verifying it at the boundary is not always possible: at $E_t = 49$ the
edge of the range is $2^{1.4 \times 10^{23}}$, which no exact arithmetic can
construct. Above roughly $E_t = 30$ the range is a fact about the encoding rather
than a measured quantity, and is reported as such.





\subsection{Which first principles}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-38-firstprinciples.png}
\caption{Canon plate. A static optimisation, not a conservation law: maximise precision subject to covering the named range, and the percentile chosen fixes the field width.}
\label{fig:canon-firstprinciples}
\end{figure}

\label{sec:principles}

The width rule has been stated as a rule. It is not one: it is the solution of a
constrained maximisation, and naming the constraint is what makes the rest of the
paper follow rather than accumulate.

\paragraph{The rule is a Karush--Kuhn--Tucker condition.} Write the design problem
directly. Maximise precision subject to a position budget and a range that must be
covered:
\[
  \max_{E,M} \; M
  \quad\text{s.t.}\quad
  1 + E + M = N,
  \qquad
  r^{E} \ge b+1 .
\]
With multipliers $\lambda$ on the budget and $\mu$ on the range,
$\mathcal{L} = M + \lambda(N-1-E-M) + \mu\bigl(E - \log_r(b+1)\bigr)$ gives
$\partial_M \mathcal{L} = 1-\lambda = 0$ and $\partial_E \mathcal{L} = -\lambda+\mu = 0$,
so $\lambda = \mu = 1$. Because $\mu > 0$, complementary slackness makes the range
constraint \emph{active}: at the optimum the exponent is exactly as wide as the
range demands and not one position wider. That statement is
Theorem~\ref{thm:optimal}, now derived rather than asserted. The multiplier is
also the shadow price, and $\lambda = 1$ says a position of exponent costs exactly
a position of mantissa --- the exchange rate is unity because the budget is linear.

\paragraph{Why three and not two is a 1950s result, not ours.} The radix economy
$\rho(r) = r/\ln r$ is minimised at $r = e$. Ternary sits $0.46\%$ above that
optimum and binary $6.15\%$ above it. We claim no credit for this; what the present
work contributes is where it applies, which turns out to be the whole
reconciliation of our two axes:
\[
  \underbrace{r}_{\text{cost per position}} \times \underbrace{\log_r V}_{\text{positions}} .
\]
The number axis counts only the second factor, and there $\log_3$ against $\log_2$
gives the slack $0.3691\,E$ of Corollary~\ref{cor:slack} in real positions at every
$E$ --- in whole trits the same corollary gives $\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}}$, which is
zero at $E \in \{1,2\}$. The fabric axis restores the first factor, and packing an
$E_t$-trit field into binary fabric charges $\lceil E_t\log_2 3\rceil$ bits, whose
utilisation is the $\rho \in (1/2,1]$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree}, which is why
BNF16 and TNF16 synthesise within $1\%$ of one another. The factor
$\kappa(3)/\kappa(2) = 1.68$ belongs to the scale-radix axis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:scaleradix} and is not a packing charge; by
Corollary~\ref{cor:worth} the two must not be substituted for one another. Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:slack} are not in tension: they are the two factors of one
product, reported separately.

\paragraph{What is derivation and what is only resemblance.} Three connections are
literal and are used as such: the multiplier argument above; radix economy, which
fixes the exponent's radix; and Kraft's inequality, which bounds the taper in
Theorem~\ref{thm:floor}. Two resemblances are worth naming only so they are not
mistaken for arguments. The variational form recalls least action, but there is no
time and no trajectory here --- the problem is a static optimisation, and the
resemblance carries no consequence. And $1+E+M=N$ is a budget, not a conservation
law: positions are allocated by a designer, not withheld by nature. We state the
distinction because a physical-sounding derivation would be doing rhetorical work
that the multiplier argument already does honestly.

\paragraph{The standard falls out --- at one choice of percentile, and not at
others.} Applied to the block axis the multiplier argument returns an element
format, but which one depends on how much of the block distribution is protected,
and that choice was left unstated in an earlier version of this section.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{The width rule on the block axis. The measured within-block span ---
SmolLM2-135M weights, block 32, span read as $\log_2(a_{\max}/\mathrm{median})$
per \texttt{research/block/block\_tnf.py}; record
\texttt{measurements/blockpct\_2026-08-20.json} --- sets
$E^{*} = \lceil \log_2 b \rceil$, and the answer moves with the percentile
protected.}
\label{tab:blockpct}
\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
\toprule
percentile protected & span, binades & $E^{*}$ & element \\
\midrule
50th (median) & 1.89 & 1 & E1M2 \\
90th & 2.45 & 2 & E2M1 \\
99th & 3.04 & 2 & E2M1 \\
99.9th & 3.75 & 2 & E2M1 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Protecting the median returns E1M2; protecting the 90th percentile or beyond
returns E2M1, which is the element the OCP microscaling specification fixes. The
correct statement is therefore a threshold rather than a derivation: \emph{the
width rule returns the MX element for any design protecting the 90th percentile
of blocks or more, and a different element below that.} Which is right is a
question about how many blocks may be allowed to saturate, not one settled from
first principles, and we say so rather than quoting the agreeing case alone.

\paragraph{} Applied to the block axis this stops being
abstract. The within-block span of a trained network's weights, measured before
any format is chosen, is $1.89$ binades at the median and $3.04$ at the 99th
percentile. The optimum is $E^{*} = \lceil \log_2 b \rceil$, so protecting the
median returns E1M2 and protecting the 99th percentile returns \emph{E2M1} ---
which is the element the OCP microscaling specification fixes. The standard is not
reproduced by coincidence: it is the unique solution of the multiplier problem at
the tail these weights actually have. Theorem~\ref{thm:regret} says which of the
two should win, since under-sizing pays on the tail linearly while over-sizing pays
logarithmically on every value, and Section~\ref{sec:blockrelated} reports the
measurement that decides it.
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-65-derivationbounda.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The paper distinguishes literal derivations from suggestive analogies: the format split is a static design budget, not a least-action trajectory or a conservation law.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-65derivationbounda}
\end{figure}



\subsection{The width rule is an optimisation with one solution}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-39-widthrule.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The coverage constraint binds, so the measured span selects one exponent width; the gain is per position and the packing toll is per bit.}
\label{fig:canon-widthrule}
\end{figure}

\label{sec:widthrule}

Theorem~\ref{thm:coverage} compares against a given competitor. Stated as a
design problem it selects a member outright, which is what makes the rule
falsifiable: it names a winner before any measurement.

\begin{theorem}[Optimal member for a named range]
\label{thm:optimal}
Let a workload visit $b$ binades and let it be \emph{centred}: a scale is applied
which carries its binade indices into the window the exponent field spans about
unity. Among all members of $\mathcal{F}_N$ that
represent it without overflow, the effective mantissa is maximised uniquely at
\[
  E_t^{*} = \bigl\lceil \log_3 (b+1) \bigr\rceil,
  \qquad
  M^{*} = N - 1 - \bigl\lceil \log_3 (b+1) \bigr\rceil .
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Under centring the placement of the window is free and representability
constrains only how many binades it must hold, so it requires $3^{E_t}-2 \ge b$,
i.e.\ $E_t \ge \log_3(b+2)$, and
$E_t$ is an integer, so $E_t \ge E_t^{*}$. By the width rule $M = N-1-E_t$ is
strictly decreasing in $E_t$, so the constraint binds and the minimiser is unique.
\end{proof}

The centring hypothesis is not a formality, and it is the one place where this
rule can be misapplied, so we state what replaces it when no scale is present.

\begin{proposition}[Uncentred workloads are sized by position, not by span]
\label{prop:uncentred}
Without a scale the exponent window is fixed symmetrically about unity: the
representable binade indices are $-(\Delta-1) \dots +(\Delta-1)$ with
$\Delta = (3^{E_t}-1)/2$. Representability then constrains
$\max(|e_{\min}|,|e_{\max}|)$ rather than $b$, and the smallest admissible
exponent width is
\[
  E_t^{\dagger} = \bigl\lceil \log_3\bigl(2\max(|e_{\min}|,|e_{\max}|)+3\bigr) \bigr\rceil
  \;\ge\; E_t^{*},
\]
with equality exactly when the visited span is symmetric about unity.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The offset field takes the values $0 \dots 3^{E_t}-1$; the top row is reserved for
the special value and the bottom for zero, leaving $3^{E_t}-2$ rows carrying
binade indices offset by $\Delta$, hence the window $-(\Delta-1) \dots +(\Delta-1)$.
Containment of the visited indices requires $\Delta-1 \ge \max(|e_{\min}|,|e_{\max}|)$,
which rearranges to $3^{E_t} \ge 2\max(|e_{\min}|,|e_{\max}|)+3$ and gives
$E_t^{\dagger}$ on taking the ceiling. Since
$\max(|e_{\min}|,|e_{\max}|) \ge (b-1)/2$ we have $2\max+3 \ge b+2$, so
$E_t^{\dagger} \ge E_t^{*}$. For a span symmetric about unity,
$e \in \{-k,\dots,k\}$, we have $b=2k+1$ and the two ceilings coincide term
for term, $2\max+3 = 2k+3 = b+2$, so they agree. For any
asymmetric span the inequality $\max > (b-1)/2$ is strict and the widths can part.
\end{proof}

The gap is not hypothetical, and it was found by measurement rather than by
reading. The logistic map $x \mapsto 3.9\,x(1-x)$ started at $x_0 = 0.4$ visits
$b=4$ binades, $2^{-4}$ up to $2^{-1}$, for which Theorem~\ref{thm:optimal}
returns $E_t^{*}=2$. But $\Delta(2)=4$, so the window of TNF$(2,10)$ reaches only
$2^{-3}$: $24$ of $400$ iterates clamp to the smallest normal, with relative
errors up to $28.6\%$ and a mean of $1.10\times10^{-2}$. The corrected width
$E_t^{\dagger}=3$ clamps nothing and attains a mean relative error of
$3.25\times10^{-4}$, the smallest of the four members at this width
\textbf{[measured --- software]}. The conclusion of Theorem~\ref{thm:optimal}
therefore survives, but only once its representability condition is read off the
window instead of off the count.

\paragraph{A scale is not a centring, and an earlier draft of this paragraph said
it was.} Every application of the rule in this paper carries a scale, and it is
tempting to read the scale as discharging the hypothesis. It does not. The scales
here are set from the block or tensor maximum --- \texttt{block-max scaling}, the
convention stated in the block harness --- which \emph{top-aligns} the visited
span rather than centring it, and top-alignment is the worst case for a window
fixed about unity.

\begin{corollary}[Top-aligned scaling wastes half the exponent window]
\label{cor:topaligned}
Let a scale carry the largest magnitude of the workload into the top representable
binade, and let the workload visit $b$ binades. Then the smallest admissible
exponent width is $\lceil \log_3(2b+1) \rceil$; it never falls below $E_t^{*}$ and
exceeds it for $133$ of the first $200$ values of $b$. The positive half of the
window, $\Delta-1$ binade indices, is unreachable by construction, and centring
the scale instead of top-aligning it recovers $\log_3 2 = 0.6309$ trits of budget.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
After scaling, the visited indices are $-(b-1) \ldots 0$. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:uncentred} containment requires $\Delta - 1 \ge b-1$ with
$\Delta = (3^{E_t}-1)/2$, that is $3^{E_t} \ge 2b+1$, giving the stated width.
Since $2b+1 \ge b+2$ for $b \ge 1$ and the ceiling of $\log_3$ is non-decreasing,
the width never falls below $E_t^{*} = \lceil\log_3(b+2)\rceil$; that it strictly
exceeds it for $133$ of $b = 1 \ldots 200$ is by enumeration. The indices
$+1 \ldots +(\Delta-1)$ receive no workload, so $\Delta$ of the $2\Delta-1$ usable
rows carry values and the usable fraction $\Delta/(2\Delta-1)$ falls from
$57.1\%$ at $E_t=2$ through $52.0\%$, $50.6\%$, $50.2\%$ to $1/2$. The budget
recovered by centring is $\log_3\bigl((2b+1)/(b+1)\bigr) \to \log_3 2$.
\end{proof}

No figure reported elsewhere moves nonetheless, and for a reason worth stating
rather than for the reason first supposed. Those figures report task error, and
representability is sufficient for zero clamping but not necessary for small task
error. The eight-bit sweep below records the opposite failure on the same weights:
there the measured optimum is one step \emph{narrower} than $E_t^{*}$, let alone
than $\lceil\log_3(2b+1)\rceil$. What binds on a weight tensor is the energy in the
tail, not representability. The correction bites where representability is the
operative constraint, which is the raw unscaled trajectory measured above, and
Corollary~\ref{cor:topaligned} says what a harness should do about it: centre the
scale, and the window stops paying for a half it cannot reach.

The rule therefore has no free parameter once the workload is named, and naming
the workload is a measurement rather than a choice. Section~\ref{sec:ternarynet}
uses this as a prediction: the binade span of a network's weights is measured
first, $E_t^{*}$ is computed from it, and only then is perplexity evaluated across
the whole family. A different winner would falsify the rule.

\begin{theorem}[Regret of a misnamed range]
\label{thm:regret}
Under the centring hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:optimal}, sizing for $b'$ when
the workload visits $b$ costs
\[
  R =
  \begin{cases}
    \lceil\log_3(b'+2)\rceil - \lceil\log_3(b+2)\rceil \ \text{mantissa bits}, & b' > b,\\[2pt]
    \text{overflow on a set of measure } 1 - \tfrac{b'}{b}, & b' < b .
  \end{cases}
\]
Both branches are non-negative as written.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:optimal} the trit budget needed to cover $b$ binades is
$E_t^{*}(b) = \lceil\log_3(b+2)\rceil$, and the width rule $1 + E_t + M = N$ fixes
the word, so at a fixed $N$ every trit spent on the exponent is a mantissa bit
forgone, one for one. Sizing for $b' > b$ therefore spends
$E_t^{*}(b') - E_t^{*}(b)$ trits that the visited range never uses, which is the
first branch, and it is non-negative because $E_t^{*}$ is non-decreasing. For
$b' < b$ the format's reach is $b'$ of the $b$ binades the workload visits; on a
log-uniform workload the density is uniform in $\log_2|x|$, hence uniform across
binades, so the uncovered share is the binade fraction $1 - b'/b$ and every value
in it overflows. The ratio $\log_3(b'+2)/\log_3(b+2)$ is the share of the
\emph{trit budget}, not of the workload measure --- at $b = 80$, $b' = 26$ the two
read $0.24$ and $0.675$ --- and an earlier revision of this theorem stated the
former where the latter belongs. Only the latter is an overflow measure. The first
branch grows like $\log_3$ of the range ratio, the second is a share of the span
and is bounded by $1$ however large the shortfall, so a fixed additional cost buys
unbounded protection in the first case and none in the second.
\end{proof}

The sign convention is worth stating explicitly: over-sizing spends the surplus
exponent trits that the smaller range would not have needed, so the first branch
subtracts the required width from the chosen one and not the reverse. The penalty
is asymmetric: over-sizing costs precision logarithmically in the range,
under-sizing costs a share of the visited span that is not logarithmic in it, so
when the visited range is uncertain the rule should round up.

The asymmetry is worth naming because it contradicts the intuition that a wider
exponent is the conservative choice. It \emph{is} conservative --- but the price is
paid in every value the format ever represents, whereas the price of being too
narrow is paid only on the tail. Which dominates is an empirical question about
the tail's weight, not a matter of taste.

\begin{corollary}[The two radix decisions do not cancel, and must not be added]
\label{cor:worth}
Two distinct quantities are attached to the word \emph{ternary} in this section and
they belong to different axes of Corollary~\ref{cor:determined}. On the axis of the
\emph{exponent's encoding}, ternary buys $\Delta = E(1-\log_3 2) = 0.3691\,E$
