\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Three observations, in decreasing order of comfort.

The wins are where the rule says they should be. Inverse-square denominators in
raw SI reach $D = 77$ and give the largest ratio in the paper, $4.00$; raw
gravitational forces give $2.32$; unnormalised likelihood products give $2.54$ at
sixteen cells; importance weights give $1.46$. None of these is a normalised
quantity, and that is the point --- they are exactly the quantities a numerical
code normalises away before it is considered well written.

The failures have teeth, which is what makes the threshold a threshold rather
than a decoration. Gamma variates at $D = 8.91$ sit just below the sixteen-cell
threshold and lose, at $0.865$. Boltzmann factors at $D = 17.23$ qualify at
sixteen cells and \emph{fail} at thirty-two, at $0.757$, because the thirty-two
cell threshold is $21.9$ and $17.23$ is below it; Monte-Carlo partial products
behave the same way. A rule that can be failed by moving to a wider format is
falsifiable in a way that a monotone claim would not be.

The range condition does most of the excluding. Four of the eleven workloads ---
Newton products, Boltzmann factors at room temperature in raw joules, the SI
constants themselves, wide-shape gamma variates --- have log-magnitude spans from
$54$ to $3342$ binades and leave the reach of both rungs entirely.
\texttt{takum} represents three of them at low accuracy without loss;
\texttt{boltzmann\_300K}, at $3342$ binades, exceeds even \texttt{takum}'s
$\pm 255$ --- $376$ of $400$ samples fall outside it at both rungs. This is
the honest shape of the result: TNF is the more accurate of the two inside a
window it must be selected into, and \texttt{takum} is the format that never has
to be selected. Which of those is preferable is a property of the deployment, not
of the format, and that is the argument for a selection procedure rather than for
a winner.

\subsection{\texttt{takum} has no range disadvantage, and we withdraw the framing that said it had}
\label{sec:takumrange}

While building the gating above we measured the representable range of the
comparison format by probing our own \texttt{takum} oracle rather than by reading
its parameterisation, and the result corrects a framing this paper carried
earlier. An exhaustive decode enumeration of all $65{,}536$ \texttt{takum16}
codes gives smallest positive $1.84 \times 10^{-77}$ and largest finite
$5.61 \times 10^{76}$ --- $\pm 255$ binades, reciprocal-symmetric ends
($1/\text{max} \approx \text{min}$), exactly the published reach of the
\emph{linear} takum --- the base-2 variant the takum paper defines in its
``Linear Takums'' section alongside the headline logarithmic
base-$\sqrt{\mathrm{e}}$ takum,
whose own reach is $\sqrt{\mathrm{e}}^{\pm 255}$, about $\pm 184$ binades;
our oracle and libtakum's default type implement the linear variant --- the
monotone extreme codes of \texttt{takum32} decode to $2^{\pm 255}$ as well, so
the reach is \emph{identical} for \texttt{takum16} and \texttt{takum32}: the
width buys precision, not reach. (An earlier revision of this paragraph printed
$3.45 \times 10^{-77}$ and $2.07 \times 10^{180}$, ``about $\pm 599$
binades''; neither end reproduces from the shipped oracle --- the encode
saturates at $2^{255}$ and underflows below $2^{-255}$, and the quoted minimum
is $2^{-254}$, twice the true one, which is a band edge rather than the code
space's edge. The numbers are corrected here; the paragraph's conclusions were
drawn from the ordering, which survives.) Against that, the $(4,8)$ rung
reaches $\pm 39$ binades and the $(5,23)$ rung $\pm 120$.

Two consequences follow. Any suggestion that a ternary exponent field buys
dynamic range relative to \texttt{takum} is withdrawn; at every width we compared,
\texttt{takum} reaches further, and in no row of Table~\ref{tab:workloads} does
\texttt{takum} go out of range where TNF does not. And of the earlier assumption we
worked from --- that \texttt{takum} reach is about $\pm 255$ binades and scales
with width --- the value was right and the scaling was wrong: the enumeration
above lands on $\pm 255$ exactly, constant across widths. We record it here
because an unmeasured number that shaped a design decision is a defect even
when it turns out true, and its companion claim did not.

\paragraph{The band where any of this matters, measured.} Accumulation of 64
terms across widening log-uniform bands (\texttt{conformance/wide\_band\_bench.py},
results in \texttt{research/WIDE\_BAND\_2026-08-19.md}; the $\pm 3$-decade band
reproduces the three-way-tie bench exactly) puts numbers on the trade: inside
$\pm 11$ decades the $(4,8)$ rung, \texttt{takum16} and the true base-3
\texttt{tekum10} tie with zero failures; the $(4,8)$ rung's usable band ends
between $\pm 11$ and $\pm 13$ decades --- 50 of 60 trials failed at $\pm 13$,
all 60 from $\pm 16$ --- precisely where its span $[1.8 \times 10^{-12},
1.1 \times 10^{12}]$ says it must, while \texttt{tekum10} (span $\pm 87$
decades by code-space scan) and \texttt{takum16} ($\pm 77$) finish every trial
out to $\pm 30$ decades at $10^{-2}$-class error. The qualifier the measurement
adds: \texttt{takum16}, a binary format, degrades exactly as gracefully as
base-3 \texttt{tekum}, so wide-band robustness is an argument for
\emph{tapering}, not for radix 3.

\subsection{A boundary contract, and the gate that now enforces it}
\label{sec:boundarygate}

The oracle defect described below was found at the underflow boundary, and it
survived every gate we had because the conformance vectors sample the interior of
each rung densely and the two range ends barely at all. A defect that hides where
the tests do not look will recur, so the boundary is now stated as an explicit
contract and checked as a required gate.

The contract is ten rules: both signed zero rows decode to zero with clean
payloads; the special row decodes to signed infinity or NaN and is reported as
special; the smallest normal and the largest finite round-trip exactly and are
fixpoints of re-encoding; every mantissa of the two end binades round-trips;
underflow ties to zero with the sign preserved and never produces a negative code
word; overflow occurs above the largest finite plus half an ulp of the top binade
and not below it; addition and multiplication commute with one operand pinned at a
range end; and the end binades are monotone in the code word. The check is
exhaustive over all $2^M$ mantissas of both end binades where $M \le 16$, which
covers TNF4, TNF8 and TNF16 completely, and deterministically sampled at about
four thousand points per binade above that --- $2^{115}$ mantissas cannot be
enumerated and we do not claim to have enumerated them.

Run over all nine rungs of both ladder versions, the gate performs $889{,}040$
checks and all of them pass \emph{[measured, software]}. It found one defect on
its first run, in the check rather than in the oracle: the overflow probe expected
the largest finite magnitude itself to overflow, which is off by half an ulp. We
report that because a boundary test that is wrong at the boundary is the failure
mode this whole subsection exists to prevent, and because it is evidence that the
gate is sensitive at the place it claims to be.

\subsection{A cost sweep that can overturn the cost model}
\label{sec:costsweep}

The cost model of Section~\ref{sec:variantB} is fitted on post-route rows with
$M \le 25$, and this paper already states two defects in it: the two sixteen-cell
rows at $M = 9$ and $M = 11$ are mutually inconsistent with any smooth monotone
model, and the widest measured rungs grow far more slowly than the quadratic term
predicts. Both are questions about the flow, not about the format, so both can be
settled by measuring more of the plane through one flow.

A generator emits $104$ synthesis arms over $52$ points of $(E_t, M)$, with
$E_t \in \{2,\dots,6\}$ and $M$ from $1$ to $33$, each arm an adder and a
multiplier behind an identical wrapper. No new arithmetic is written: a TNF
exponent field is a binary field of $\lceil E_t \log_2 3 \rceil$ cells holding a
ternary-derived offset, the datapath never sees a trit, and so the audited
parameterised GF adder and multiplier cores are instantiated at the sweep
parameters. What the sweep measures is therefore the cost of the field layout,
which is exactly the quantity the $\varphi$ rule chooses, and nothing else.

The accompanying analysis is written to be able to contradict us. It fits the
quadratic and a linear model on the same rows and compares them by adjusted
$R^2$, so ``the $M^2$ term survives'' becomes a measured statement; it reports the
residual of the published coefficients on the new rows separately from any refit,
because that residual is the only number that says whether the published model
still holds; and arms that fail routing are listed as routing-pending and
excluded from every fit rather than imputed, because a fabric limit is not a cost
measurement. Two arms exist purely to re-measure $M = 9$ and $M = 11$ side by side,
and if they reconcile the paper will have to retract its own caveat.

We state plainly what is not here. The sweep is a prepared, committed workflow;
at the time of writing it has not been run, and no row of it is reported anywhere
in this paper. Post-route numbers from it would be \emph{[measured, CI synthesis]}
and yosys estimates \emph{[modelled]}; neither is a hardware measurement. Hardware
means a UART transcript from the AX7203 board's XC7A200T FPGA fabric
(\texttt{xc7a200tfbg484-2}, IDCODE \texttt{0x13636093}) alongside a green
continuous-integration URL and an artefact digest, and no amount of synthesis
substitutes for it.

\subsection{A representation advantage is not yet a computation advantage}
\label{sec:downstream}

Every accuracy number above this point measures \emph{representation}: a value is
encoded, decoded, and the round-trip error is reported. The strongest objection
left to this paper is that the quantity a user cares about is the outcome of a
computation, and that a format can hold values well and still deliver a worse
answer. That objection cannot be answered by another round-trip sweep, so we ran
one task and measured the task's result.

\paragraph{The task.} A maximum-a-posteriori estimate of the solar gravitational
parameter $\mu = GM_{\odot}$ from a sum of $66$ Gaussian log-likelihoods over
orbital accelerations, in raw SI units and without normalisation --- so the
intermediate magnitudes span the region Section~\ref{sec:nolanding} identified as
the one where the condition is satisfiable, rather than being rescaled out of it.
Radii run from $1.1\times 10^{15}$ to $5.8\times 10^{17}$\,m; the seeded truth is
$\mu = 1.32712440018\times 10^{20}$; the per-observation noise is $0.5\%$ of the
true acceleration; the optimiser is a golden-section search over $\log\mu$ run for
$72$ iterations to a bracket width of $7.1\times 10^{-15}$. Each format under test
stores the observations and the per-term residuals; the accumulator is
\texttt{float32} in every case, held fixed across all candidates so that the
summation path cannot be the thing being compared. The
\texttt{float64} run is the reference solution, not a competitor.

\begin{table}[t]\centering
\caption{Outcome of one numerical task rather than a round-trip error
\textbf{[measured --- software]}. Iterations are those needed for the estimate to
settle to a relative change below $10^{-6}$; all runs were carried to $72$.
The reference column is the \texttt{float64} solution of the same problem, so
column three measures agreement with that solution and not distance from the
seeded truth. \emph{Data:} \texttt{measurements/tnf\_downstream\_bayesian\_si\_2026-08-13.json}; regenerated by \texttt{recompute\_downstream\_table.py}}
\label{tab:downstream}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
format & $\hat\mu$ (SI) & rel.\ error vs \texttt{float64} & iterations to $10^{-6}$ \\
\midrule
\texttt{float64} (reference) & $1.3272722754\times 10^{20}$ & --- & $28$ \\
\texttt{binary32}            & $1.3272718991\times 10^{20}$ & $2.84\times 10^{-7}$ & $28$ \\
\textbf{TNF32 $(5,23)$}      & $1.3272596725\times 10^{20}$ & $9.50\times 10^{-6}$ & $\mathbf{21}$ \\
\texttt{takum32}             & $1.3272521518\times 10^{20}$ & $1.52\times 10^{-5}$ & $27$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{What it shows, and only that.} TNF32 lands $1.60\times$ closer to the
\texttt{float64} solution than \texttt{takum32} does, and settles in $21$
iterations against that format's $27$. This is a task-level number in this paper
about the result of a computation rather than about the storage of a value, and it
points the same way as the representation measurements on a workload that meets
the condition of Section~\ref{sec:window}. That is the whole of the positive
claim.

\paragraph{Four boundaries, each of which a reader is entitled to press.}
First, \texttt{binary32} beats both by a factor of thirty-three on the same
metric, and nothing here recommends either format over an IEEE single where one is
available --- the comparison that is live is TNF against the tapered class at the
same width, not against \texttt{binary32}. Second, \texttt{takum32}'s estimate is
in fact slightly \emph{closer} to the seeded $\mu$ ($9.63\times 10^{-5}$ against
$1.02\times 10^{-4}$); that reflects where the perturbed sample happened to fall
relative to the truth and is not a property of either format, which is why the
table is stated against the \texttt{float64} solution of the same data. Third, the
observations are synthetic \textbf{[modelled]}: the generator is deterministic
under a fixed seed and the data set is not an instrument record. Fourth, this is
one task at one rung, and an iteration count is not throughput, area or energy;
no hardware quantity follows from it. Whether the effect holds across tasks, and
whether it holds at the $(4,8)$ rung whose threshold differs
(Table~\ref{tab:rungthr}), is not settled here. The generator, the oracles and the
result record are in the repository alongside the sweeps.

\subsection{A second task: conjugate gradients in raw scale}
\label{sec:downstream-cg}

To test the objection on a task with a different numerical structure, we solved a
$2\times2$ symmetric positive-definite linear system by conjugate gradients.
The coefficients were deliberately kept in raw, unnormalised scale,
\[
A=\begin{bmatrix}1264.196608&0.07925\\0.07925&0.0156171875\end{bmatrix},
\qquad
b=\begin{bmatrix}1560.464823776\\0.06120245\end{bmatrix}.
\]
The exact solution used to generate $b$ was $(1.2345,-2.3456)$. Matrix entries,
vectors, products and updates were rounded in the candidate format; every
candidate dot product used a \texttt{float32} accumulator. The \texttt{float64}
run is only a reference. After the solver stopped, the residual was recomputed
in \texttt{float64} from the unrounded matrix and right-hand side. Thus the
external residual is not silently replaced by the quantised solver's internal
stopping value.

\begin{table}[t]\centering\small
\caption{Outcome of a second numerical task, conjugate-gradient solution rather
than a representation error \textbf{[measured --- software]}. The last column is
the number of solver iterations executed before the loop stopped, which happens when
the stored residual reaches exactly zero, when the stored curvature denominator
ceases to be finite and non-zero, or at the cap of $80$; the middle column is an
independently recomputed residual in \texttt{float64}. All three low-precision
candidates crossed the internal $10^{-6}$ threshold at iteration 3, so the last
column records where each solver stalled and not how fast it converged.
TNF$(4,8)$ is the rung that fits sixteen \emph{physical} cells, the same
convention under which \texttt{takum16} and \texttt{binary16} are read here; it
is not the reconciled TNF16 rung $(4,11)$, which is stated at the nominal
budget. \emph{Data:} \texttt{measurements/tnf\_downstream\_linear\_cg\_2026-08-14.json}; regenerated by \texttt{recompute\_downstream\_cg\_table.py}}
\label{tab:downstream-cg}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
format & relative solution error & external relative residual & iterations run \\
\midrule
\texttt{float64} (reference) & $8.38\times10^{-17}$ & $1.46\times10^{-16}$ & 32 \\
\texttt{binary16}            & $1.44\times10^{-3}$  & $1.01\times10^{-4}$  & 5  \\
\textbf{TNF$(4,8)$}          & $2.25\times10^{-3}$  & $1.02\times10^{-4}$  & 7  \\
\texttt{takum16}             & $2.18\times10^{-3}$  & $2.95\times10^{-4}$  & 15 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

In this experiment TNF$(4,8)$ did not beat \texttt{takum16} on solution error:
$2.25\times10^{-3}$ against $2.18\times10^{-3}$. It had the smaller externally
recomputed residual, $1.02\times10^{-4}$ against $2.95\times10^{-4}$, and
\texttt{binary16} had the smallest solution error of the three low-precision
candidates at $1.44\times10^{-3}$. No general conclusion about the suitability or
superiority of any of these formats follows from one $2\times2$ SPD case; this is a
task-level observation for one matrix and one rung. The solver iteration count is
not a throughput result, and the synthetic system supplies no evidence about
energy, area or FPGA timing. The negative reading stands as written and is not
softened: on the metric this experiment was built to measure, the format did not
win. It is reported because it was run, not because it agrees.

\begin{sloppypar}
The run is a closed artefact rather than a claim about one. Command:
\texttt{python3}\ \texttt{research/}\allowbreak\texttt{arxiv\_tnf/}\allowbreak\texttt{measurements/}\allowbreak\texttt{gen\_downstream\_}\allowbreak\texttt{linear\_cg.py}
from the repository root. The record
(\texttt{measurements/}\allowbreak\texttt{tnf\_downstream\_}\allowbreak\texttt{linear\_cg\_}\allowbreak\texttt{2026-08-14.json}) carries $A$ in
raw scale, $x_{\text{true}}$, the generated $b$, $x_0 = (0,0)$, the stopping rule
verbatim, the cap of $80$, the recorded tolerance $10^{-6}$, the $\texttt{float32}$
accumulator convention, the rung $(E_t,M)=(4,8)$ with the note that it is not the
reconciled $(4,11)$, the \texttt{takum16} reference module, \texttt{git} head,
interpreter and \texttt{numpy} versions, SHA-256 of both oracles and of the
generator, and a SHA-256 of the canonical record payload (with the digest field
excluded). Re-running it on this revision reproduced
every digit of Table~\ref{tab:downstream-cg}. Unlike the frequency column discussed
in Section~\ref{sec:provenance}, this result is reproducible from the released
artefact.
\end{sloppypar}

\paragraph{A third task attempted, and a metric we disqualified.} We also ran the
logistic map $x \mapsto 3.9\,x(1-x)$, intending to score candidates by the
iteration at which the quantised trajectory departs from the \texttt{float64}
reference by more than $10^{-2}$. That metric does not survive its own control and
we therefore report no ranking from it. Of the four family members at this width,
the one with the smallest mean representation error on the trajectory, TNF$(3,9)$
at $3.25\times10^{-4}$, departs at iteration $5$, while TNF$(2,10)$, whose mean
representation error is $34$ times larger at $1.10\times10^{-2}$, survives to
iteration $13$ \textbf{[measured --- software]}. In a map with a positive Lyapunov
exponent the departure time is governed by the sign and direction of the first
rounding error rather than by its size, so the ordering it produces is not a
precision ordering. The run was not wasted: it is what exposed the missing
centring hypothesis repaired in Proposition~\ref{prop:uncentred}.

\subsection{A defect the sweep exposed in our own oracle}
\label{sec:oracledefect}

The sweeps above pushed the reference implementation far outside the range of the
conformance vectors, and that exposed a defect in it which we record here because
the vectors we published are affected. On underflow, \texttt{encode} clamped the
exponent offset to its minimum and continued; the significand term then goes
negative, the code word comes out negative, and \texttt{decode} reads that as a
large value of the opposite sign. The magnitude $2^{-40}$ at the $(4,8)$ rung
encoded to a word that decoded as $-2.32\times 10^{26}$, a relative error of
$2.6\times 10^{38}$, with no special value raised. Overflow was handled correctly,
which is why the defect survived: it was one-sided.

Since there are no subnormals in this encoding, an underflowing magnitude has
exactly two representable neighbours, zero and the smallest normal, so the repair
is to round to the nearer of the two, ties to zero, preserving the sign. With that
change the round-trip and ladder-invariant self-tests pass unchanged, and no
negative code word is produced at any rung.

The consequence for the artefacts is the part worth stating. Between $47$ and
$86$ of the $1038$ rows in each of the eighteen published vector files, five to
eight per cent, are multiplications whose expected result was recorded as a
negative code word, which no hardware can produce. Those rows were unsatisfiable.
Rather than overwrite files whose digests are cited, we bumped the vector
specification version: the affected files remain in place under
\texttt{tnf-vectors-2} and the repaired set is published alongside them as
\texttt{tnf-vectors-3}, with its own manifest of SHA-256 digests. Any transcript
claiming conformance against the older manifest should be re-read with this in
mind.

\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations-anchor}\label{sec:limits}

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-32-limits.png}
\caption{Canon plate. Limitations: the fabric, the twin oracle, and the price of range.}
\label{fig:canon-limits}
\end{figure}


\paragraph{The fabric this format is optimal on does not exist to buy.}
Theorem~\ref{thm:nofree} confines the ternary encoding's benefit to a ternary
fabric, and no such process is commercially available. Every hardware figure in
this paper is therefore a post-route estimate for binary FPGA fabric, where the
ternary exponent neither gains nor loses on the scale-state count --- the
enumeration in Section~\ref{sec:whyternary} shows it ties a binary encoding
exactly at the widths tested. It does lose on a second axis, and this revision
proves it: Theorem~\ref{thm:rangeperbit} shows a ternary exponent never buys range
per stored bit, with equality only at $t=1$. The two statements are about
different axes and both hold. The claims that survive on parts you can buy today are the fixed-field
ones: no regime codec, no exponential to
evaluate. The ternary claim is architectural and is labelled as such throughout.

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The comparison was against takum16, not tekum16 --- and the
genuine tekum comparison has since been done.} The oracle labelled tekum
decodes all $65{,}536$ 16-bit codes identically to the takum oracle; it is a
linear binary model of takum's layout. \texttt{tekum\_true\_ref} now
implements the real base-3 tekum of arXiv:2512.10964, Definitions 7--8 --- the
paper's worked example reproduces exactly and the encoding is monotone on all
$6{,}559$ tekum8 codes. Its widths count trits, so tekum16 is 16 trits
$= 25.4$ bits with $3^{16}$ codes. At equal stored width the accumulation
benchmark is a three-way tie: tekum10 ($15.85$ bits) $8.56\mathrm{e}{-3}$,
takum16 $5.70\mathrm{e}{-3}$, TNF(4,8) $5.32\mathrm{e}{-3}$ at the 16-bit
class, and $1.43/1.26/1.20\mathrm{e}{-7}$ at 32. No format wins by more than
the width it gives up.

\item \textbf{The reference oracle carried a sign defect, now fixed.} Round-tripping
$1.5$ through the parameterised oracle returned $-1.5$ at mantissa widths of 21
and 25 bits: the sign bit sat at a fixed position while the exponent mask took
more bits than the field holds, so the two overlapped. Both are now derived from
the format parameters, and TNF32's round-trip error falls from
$5.4\mathrm{e}{-1}$ --- what a systematically inverted sign averages to --- to
$5.3\mathrm{e}{-9}$.

We record how it survived, because the lesson outlives the bug. The ladder's
check was add/mul commutativity, and an inverted sign survives on both sides of
$a+b=b+a$. The check was real and blind to the class. A round-trip assertion sees
it, costs one line per probe, and now runs at all nine rungs. tekum~\cite{tekum} is a descendant of takum~\cite{takum} adapted for balanced
ternary. Our
linear oracle reconstructed it from takum's field scheme; the full per-trit
specification is now implemented from the tekum paper as
\texttt{tekum\_true\_ref} (Definitions 7--8, exact on the worked example,
monotone on all $6{,}559$ tekum8 codes). The takum oracle carried a defect of
its own --- negation flips the exponent sign, and every negative code decoded
wrongly until \#606; it now matches libtakum on $65{,}534$ of $65{,}535$ codes
--- so every takum ratio measured before that fix is superseded by the
equal-width tie of Section~\ref{sec:accuracy}.

\item \textbf{The head-to-head in hardware now exists, with caveats.} takum's
published codec RTL is VHDL~\cite{takumrtl} and was not synthesised here, so
those figures remain TNF's own. What has since been synthesised head-to-head:
\texttt{tef\_add\_full} --- sign, subtract, round-to-nearest-even, normalise
--- verified on $3{,}000$ oracle vectors plus $40$ chained accumulations of 32
terms with zero errors, at $397$ LUT / $45$ CARRY4 for TNF(4,8) against
$1{,}182$ LUT / $211$ CARRY4 for a 16-bit adder of the \emph{linear
takum-layout model}, $2.98\times$; and the first real-tekum hardware,
\texttt{tekum8\_decode.v}, exhaustively verified on all $6{,}558$ codes with
zero errors, at $542$ LUT / $96$ CARRY4 against $1$ LUT for the TNF
field-slice decode. Caveats, all of them: Yosys 0.65 \texttt{synth\_xilinx},
last stat block only (an earlier extraction summed all blocks and inflated
every figure threefold), post-synthesis with no place-and-route and no board;
the adder competitor is a structural model of takum's layout, not the
published VHDL; and on a ternary fabric tekum's trit extraction would be
wiring. What is visible in that source, and what we report as structure
rather than measurement, is this: the 16-bit codec instantiates a 725-line
generated leading-zero counter and barrel shifter --- machinery a fixed-field
format does not need for regime decoding.

\item \textbf{The range is bounded} at $\pm39$ in powers of two, roughly $\pm12$
decades, where a regime field is unbounded. Fixed fields buy the cheap datapath
and the uniform mantissa; range is the price, and workloads that need more should
raise the trit budget or use a tapered format.

\item \textbf{Accuracy above TNF32 requires exact rationals, and now uses them.}
Once the oracle defect was fixed, TNF32 measures $5.17\mathrm{e}{-9}$, flat
across the workload, which is what 25 mantissa bits should give. TNF64 carries
52 mantissa bits in the research note --- 56 once the width rule is applied ---
and 52 is exactly the significand of the IEEE double, so a double-precision
metric at that rung and above would describe the metric rather than the format.
Table~\ref{tab:law} is therefore computed in exact rationals with 1{,}200-bit
significands, which is what lets the upper rungs be quoted at all; the hardware
limit below is separate and unchanged.

\item \textbf{Five of nine rungs have post-route implementation results.} TNF4 through TNF64 fit
the device, TNF64 at $7{,}479$ LUTs and $48.20$\,MHz. TNF128 at its reconciled
$M = 119$ does not
converge in routing on this part and is not claimed; TNF256 upwards exceed a
single Artix-7 fabric entirely. Table~\ref{tab:spec} is the specification at the
width rule; Table~\ref{tab:ladder} is what was placed and routed. We keep them
apart on purpose.

\item \textbf{The shipped reference oracle is still on the research-note widths.}
The published \texttt{tnf\_ref.py} ladder table carries $(E_t,M)$ of
$(4,9)$, $(5,21)$, $(7,52)$, $(8,115)$ and upward --- the pre-reconciliation
budgets, seven of which do not satisfy $1+E_t+M=N$ --- an earlier revision of this
item said three, and the per-rung audit in Table~\ref{tab:ladderversions} says
seven. Table~\ref{tab:spec} above is
the reconciled specification, not a transcript of that file. Bringing the oracle
onto the width rule changes its conformance vectors and therefore their SHA-256
fingerprints, so it is a versioned change and is not folded in silently here.
Section~\ref{sec:variantA} describes how that versioning was carried out: both
ladders now ship as named objects with hash-pinned vector manifests, so the
research-note configuration stays reproducible instead of being overwritten. Any
figure in this paper that quotes an oracle result quotes the research-note
configuration it was actually run at, and the two must not be cited as one
artefact.

\item \textbf{One device family.} The results use Artix-7 on an open flow; they
are not a multi-corner characterisation, and an ASIC mapping will differ.

\item \textbf{The ternary-fabric argument is architectural.} No ternary process
exists to synthesise on. Section~\ref{sec:hardware} is a binary-fabric result;
the ternary claim is reasoning about regime decode and native exponent addition
and should be read as such.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-77-measurementbound.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The limitations distinguish the duplicate takum/tekum oracle, the corrected TNF32 sign defect, and the hardware range actually placed on the Artix-7.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-77measurementbound}
\end{figure}


\section*{Reproducibility}

The reference oracle, the RTL, the equivalence testbenches and the synthesis
commands are public. The toolchain is Yosys 0.65, nextpnr-xilinx 1743d0f,
Icarus Verilog 13.0 and Python 3.14 --- all open-source, none requiring a
licence, which is the point: a result nobody can reproduce without buying
something is not a public result.
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{canon/canon-tnf-54-opentoolchain.png}
\caption{Canon plate. The public oracle, RTL, tests, commands, and open-source toolchain make the reported work reproducible without a licence.}
\label{fig:canon-b4-54opentoolchain}
\end{figure}


\section*{Disclosure}

An AI coding assistant was used in preparing the software, the measurements'
tooling and this manuscript. Every figure reported here was produced by running
the named tools on hardware in the author's possession. Figures from that
toolchain are reported as post-route estimates, and where a figure is derived rather than observed it is
labelled.

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering
